The colourful antenna subtraction method Preprint: IPPP/23/63 ZU-TH 70/23
T. Gehrmann a , E.W.N. Glover b , M. Marcoli a,b

Abstract
We present a general subtraction scheme for NNLO calculations in massless QCD: the colourful antenna subtraction method . It is a reformulation of the antenna subtraction approach designed to address some of the limitations of the traditional framework, especially aiming at high-multiplicity processes. In the context of the new formalism, structures needed to locally subtract the infrared-divergent behaviour of real emission corrections are systematically inferred from virtual subtraction terms, relying on the cancellation of infrared singularities and on the correspondence between integrated and unintegrated antenna functions. We illustrate in detail how the colourful antenna subtraction method works up to NNLO. The algorithm is particularly suited to be fully automated for the generation of NNLO subtraction terms for generic processes. We employ the new formalism to assemble the subtraction terms required for the calculation of the NNLO correction to hadronic three-jet production and describe their validation procedure.



1
Introduction

The successful interpretation of particle collider data demands high-precision theoretical predictions for Standard Model cross sections. Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) effects dominate scattering events at particle colliders and need to be described accurately to reach the desired precision. Computations in QCD for high-energy collisions are performed by power series expansion in the strong coupling constant
\alpha_{s}
. According to how many terms are computed in this expansion, one obtains increasingly more accurate predictions, denominated Leading-Order (LO), Next-to-Leading-Order (NLO), Next-to-Next-to-Leading-Order (NNLO), and so forth. The complexity grows with the accuracy: the emergence of
infrared divergences
in theoretical calculations poses an obstacle to the computation of higher-order terms in the
\alpha_{s}
expansion.

LO predictions only provide a naive estimate and are not accurate enough for precision experiments. More precise calculations at NLO are required for any precision analysis. For more than two decades, fully general techniques to reach NLO accuracy have been available
Catani:1996jh
;
Frixione:1995ms
, as well as public multi-purpose computational frameworks for cross-section calculations
Alwall:2014hca
;
Sherpa:2019gpd
;
Bellm:2019zci
;
Alioli:2010xd
. However, for some observables NLO predictions are still not precise enough and NNLO calculations are needed to approach the percent-level accuracy goal. Several methods have been proposed to compute NNLO corrections
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
;
DelDuca:2016ily
;
Catani:2007vq
;
Czakon:2010td
;
Czakon:2014oma
;
Gaunt:2015pea
;
Cacciari:2015jma
;
Caola:2017dug
;
Magnea:2018hab
;
Bertolotti:2022aih
;
Devoto:2023rpv
and NNLO-accurate predictions for essentially all
2\to 2
processes at hadron colliders are nowadays available. From
2020
onwards, NNLO calculations for
2\to 3
processes have started to appear
Chawdhry:2019bji
;
Kallweit:2020gcp
;
Chawdhry:2021hkp
;
Czakon:2021mjy
;
Chen:2022ktf
;
Hartanto:2022qhh
;
Alvarez:2023fhi
;
Badger:2023mgf
;
Catani:2022mfv
;
Buonocore:2022pqq
;
Buonocore:2023ljm
, thanks to the calculation of two-loop five-point amplitudes
Chicherin:2017dob
;
Gehrmann:2018yef
;
Chicherin:2020oor
;
Abreu:2020cwb
;
Abreu:2021oya
;
Abreu:2021asb
;
Badger:2021ega
;
Badger:2021imn
;
Abreu:2023rco
. These results represent the current state-of-the-art for NNLO QCD corrections for LHC processes.

The currently available results at NNLO have been obtained proceeding on a case-by-case basis, usually demanding a considerable amount of work to extend the existing formalisms to new classes of processes. At NLO, the development of process-independent and automatable techniques led to the industrialization of NLO-accurate predictions. Nowadays, NLO computations are systematically performed using public codes for any precision analysis at the LHC, thereby raising the standards of particle phenomenology. It is foreseeable that a similar revolution could occur at NNLO if general frameworks and tools for NNLO calculations become available.

In this paper we formulate a process-independent approach to perform NNLO calculations in QCD: the
colourful antenna subtraction method
, which was originally presented in
Chen:2022ktf
for gluon scattering. The traditional antenna subtraction method
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
makes use of
antenna functions

Gehrmann-DeRidder:2004ttg
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005alt
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005svg
to assemble suitable counterterms to deal with infrared divergences up to NNLO. These antenna functions
are based on colour-ordered matrix elements describing radiation from processes with two coloured particles:
\gamma^{*}\to q\bar{q}
,
\tilde{\chi}\to\tilde{g}g
and
H\to gg
, covering the cases where the coloured particles are massless quarks and gluons. It has been successfully applied to compute the NNLO correction to a series of phenomenologically relevant processes at electron-positron, electron-proton and hadron colliders
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
;
Currie:2017tpe
;
Currie:2017eqf
;
Chen:2022clm
;
Chen:2022ktf
;
Chen:2019zmr
;
Gehrmann:2020oec
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2015wbt
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2016cdi
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2017mvr
;
Gauld:2020deh
;
Gauld:2023zlv
;
Chen:2016zka
;
Cruz-Martinez:2018rod
;
Gauld:2019yng
;
Gauld:2021ule
. However, it has been gradually extended throughout the past fifteen years to deal with new processes, demanding a substantial amount of time and manpower for each new process. Despite being quite flexible, it presents some intrinsic limitations.
In particular, as many of the proposed NNLO subtraction schemes, it scales poorly with the number of coloured particles involved in the process and much of its simplicity is lost when dealing with contributions beyond the so-called
leading-colour
approximation
Currie:2013vh
where one cannot directly rely on colour connections of squared amplitudes as the guiding principle for the construction of the subtraction terms, especially at high multiplicities.

The colourful antenna subtraction method derived here is a reformulation of the antenna subtraction approach designed to systematise the construction of the real-emission subtraction term. The main idea behind it is to exploit the predictability of the singularity structure of virtual amplitudes in colour space to straightforwardly construct the virtual subtraction terms in a completely general way.
This helps overcome the aforementioned limitations of the traditional antenna subtraction scheme in two ways.
First, the complete structure of colour correlations among external QCD particles is retained by working in colour-space, making the construction of subtraction terms for
all
colour layers straightforward. This improves on the traditional approach using colour-decomposed antenna functions, which works best only for the leading-colour contribution.
Secondly, we exploit the correspondence between integrated and unintegrated antenna functions and the known infrared structure of the virtual subtraction terms to infer the subtraction terms for the real emission corrections. This strategy goes in the opposite direction with respect to the typical procedure implemented in infrared subtraction schemes, in which the real emission subtraction term is constructed first, and then analytically integrated to yield the virtual subtraction term. Nonetheless it is firmly supported by the same principle: the cancellation of infrared singularities between real and virtual corrections. The construction of local subtraction terms for real radiation starting from the infrared factorization properties of virtual amplitudes has also been investigated in
Magnea:2018ebr
.

The colourful antenna approach is fully compatible with other (parallel) attempts to refine the antenna subtraction scheme by building the antenna functions directly from a list of desired infrared limits
Braun-White:2023sgd
;
Braun-White:2023zwd
;
Fox:2023bma
. This so-called
designer antenna
approach avoids the need to decompose the antenna functions into sub-antennae, it streamlines (and reduces the size of) the subtraction terms by avoiding the introduction of spurious limits that are inevitably present in the matrix-element based antennae when applied to processes with multiple coloured particles. So far, designer antenna functions and their integrated counterparts have been derived for use in electron-positron annihilation, electron-hadron and hadron-hadron collisions at NLO
Braun-White:2023sgd
;
Fox:2023bma
, and at NNLO for electron-positron annihilation
Braun-White:2023sgd
;
Braun-White:2023zwd
. In the future, one can imagine an optimal approach in which the colourful antenna subtraction method proposed here is supplemented by the use of designer antenna functions, and vice versa.

The outline of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
we give a brief overview of the basic ingredients and structures of the traditional antenna subtraction method. Section
3
, after reviewing the colour space formalism and the infrared singularity structure of QCD virtual amplitudes, introduces
integrated dipoles
as operators in colour space, which play a fundamental role in the formalism we propose. In Sections
4
and
5
we present the colourful antenna subtraction method, respectively at NLO and NNLO, supplemented by the appendices. The application of the new colourful formalism for the construction of NNLO subtraction terms for three-jet production at hadron colliders is then discussed in Section
6
. Concluding remarks and an outlook are presented in Section
7
.

2
Basics of antenna subtraction

The antenna subtraction method has been developed in detail in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
;
Currie:2013vh
. We summarize its essential features in the following.

The core ingredients of the antenna subtraction method are the
antenna functions
. These objects capture the singular behaviour of matrix elements in the presence of unresolved emissions and are directly extracted from colour-ordered squared matrix elements for simple processes involving the radiation of QCD particles between a pair of hard radiators
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2004ttg
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005alt
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005svg
. According to the partonic species of the hard radiators, antenna functions are classified as quark-antiquark, quark-gluon and gluon-gluon antenna functions.

An unintegrated
\ell
-loop antenna function is a function of
n
partonic momenta, a subset
I
of which are in the initial state, generically indicated as:

X_{n,\left\{i\right\}_{i\in I}}^{\ell}(j_{1},\dots,j_{n}).

(1)

Configurations with zero, one or two partons in the initial state are respectively denoted by final-final (FF), initial-final (IF) and initial-initial (II) antenna functions. Different partonic arrangements are described by different antenna functions, characterized with a specific upper-case letter replacing the generic
X
. At one loop,
\ell=1
,
antenna functions containing a closed fermion loop are indicated by
a
hat
(
\widehat{\phantom{X}}
), and antenna functions corresponding to the subleading-colour virtual configurations are indicated
with a
tilde
(
\widetilde{\phantom{X}}
). Some of the antenna functions are further split into so-called
sub-antennae
. This is done for several reasons such as the identification of two hard radiators, the separation of different reduced matrix elements or the separation of different momentum mappings. Typically sub-antennae have a more complicated structure than the full result. Sub-antennae are denoted with the same notation as full ones, but with lower-case letters or with a dedicated subscript.

Exploiting the exact factorization of the phase space
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005alt
;
Daleo:2006xa
;
Daleo:2009yj
;
Boughezal:2010mc
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2012too
, antenna functions can be analytically integrated in dimensional regularization to obtain their integrated counterparts. Integrated antenna functions are denoted as:

\mathcal{X}_{n,\left\{i\right\}_{i\in I}}^{\ell}(s_{j_{1}\dots j_{n}}),

(2)

where
s_{j_{q}\dots j_{n}}
is the invariant mass of the considered partonic system:

s_{j_{1}\dots j_{n}}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\lambda_{j}p_{j}\right)^{2},

(3)

with
\lambda_{j}=+1
for final-state partons and
\lambda_{j}=-1
for initial-state ones.

All the integrated and unintegrated antenna functions and sub-antennae used in this paper were derived in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
;
Glover:2010kwr
;
Daleo:2006xa
;
Daleo:2009yj
;
Boughezal:2010mc
;
Gehrmann:2011wi
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2012too
.

2.1
Structure of antenna subtraction

In the following we review the infrastructure of the antenna subtraction method and of the subtraction counterterms which are constructed to remove the infrared singularities in different layers of a calculation. The detailed construction of the subtraction counterterms is described in Section
4
and
5
.

2.1.1
Subtraction at NLO

The NLO QCD correction to an
n
-jet partonic cross section with parton species
a
and
b
in the initial state is given by:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=\int_{n}\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)+\int_{n+1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}},

(4)

where
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
respectively represent the virtual and real corrections, while
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
is the NLO mass factorization counterterm. The symbol
\int_{n}
indicates an integration over a
n
-particle phase space. The NLO cross section in (
4
), despite being well defined and finite, is not suitable for numerical integration in this form, due to the emergence of infrared singularities. These cancel in the final result, but a proper subtraction procedure is needed to separately remove the singularities in the real and virtual corrections and make both integrals in (
4
) computable with numerical methods.

In the context of antenna subtraction, this is achieved constructing a real subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}

Currie:2013vh
, which locally removes the singular behaviour of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
in the infrared (IR) limits and can be analytically integrated over the phase space of the unresolved radiation. For the regularization of the IR singularities, we rely on dimensional regularization with the customary choice
d=4-2\epsilon
. After this integration,

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
serves as ingredient to
the virtual subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
, which cancels the explicit
\epsilon
-poles of the virtual correction and contains the mass factorization contribution. The NLO cross section can then be reformulated as
Currie:2013vh
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=\int_{n}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right]+\int_{n+1}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right],

(5)

with

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}.

(6)

At NLO the mass factorization counterterm is given by:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{c,d}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}},

(7)

where
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
represent the momentum fractions transferred to the hard process, while
\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)
denotes the NLO mass factorization kernel:

\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)=\Gamma^{(1)}_{ca,\text{full}}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{db}\delta(1-x_{2})+\Gamma^{(1)}_{db,\text{full}}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{ca}\delta(1-x_{1}).

(8)

which can be organized into different terms corresponding to different colour factors:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq,\text{full}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x\right),

(9)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq,\text{full}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x\right),

(10)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg,\text{full}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x\right),

(11)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg,\text{full}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x\right)+N_{f}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x}\right).

(12)

The NLO mass factorization terms are directly related to regularized LO Altarelli-Parisi splitting kernels
Altarelli:1977zs
:

\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab,\text{full}}\left(x\right)=-\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}P^{0}_{ab}(x),

(13)

where

\displaystyle P^{0}_{qq}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)p^{0}_{qq}(x),

(14)

\displaystyle P^{0}_{gq}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)p^{0}_{gq}(x),

(15)

\displaystyle P^{0}_{qg}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p^{0}_{qg}(x),

(16)

\displaystyle P^{0}_{gg}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}p^{0}_{gg}(x)+N_{f}\hat{p}^{0}_{gg}(x).

(17)

2.1.2
Subtraction at NNLO

The NNLO QCD correction to an
n
-jet cross section is given by:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{n}\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right)

(18)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{n+1}\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right)+\int_{n+2}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RR}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

where
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
represents the double-virtual correction,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
the real-virtual correction and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RR}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
the double-real correction. The mass factorization counterterm at this perturbative order is split into two terms associated with
n
- and
(n+1)
-particle final states, respectively
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

As in the NLO case, the quantity in (
18
) cannot be computed directly with numerical methods. The singular behaviour of both the double-real and real-virtual corrections in infrared limits has to be subtracted and the explicit poles in the double-virtual and real-virtual matrix elements need to be removed properly. To achieve this, the NNLO cross section is rewritten in the context of antenna subtraction as
Currie:2013vh
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{n}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]

(19)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{n+1}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{n+2}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RR}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

where the subtracted quantities are the double-virtual, the real-virtual and the double-real subtraction term. These contributions have the following form
Currie:2013vh
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\int_{2}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,.

(20)

The double-real subtraction term has been decomposed into two contributions which contain single and double unresolved IR limits:
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. In the real-virtual subtraction term,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
cancels the implicit singular behaviour of the real-virtual correction in single unresolved limits. The remaining terms in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which are not mass factorization counterterms, are obtained via analytical integration of the aforementioned contributions, over the phase space of a single or double unresolved emission. These terms cancel explicit
\epsilon
-poles in the virtual corrections.

Within the traditional antenna subtraction approach
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013dwa
, the infrastructure above is constructed starting from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which are obtained by studying the infrared behaviour of the double-real and real-virtual matrix elements. Afterwards, analytical integrations are performed to complete the subtraction. Several adjustments are needed during this process to ensure the removal of spurious divergences and prevent the oversubtraction of singular behaviour. Examples of the typical structures appearing in each layer can be found in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
;
Glover:2010kwr
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2011jwo
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2012dog
;
Currie:2013vh
.

At NNLO, we have two different contributions to the mass factorization counterterm: the double-virtual and the real-virtual mass factorization terms. The real-virtual mass factorization counterterm is given by
Currie:2013vh
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{c,d}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right),

(21)

where the required mass factorization kernels are the ones used at NLO.

The double-virtual mass factorization counterterm reads
Currie:2013vh
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\sum_{c,d}\Bigg\{\Bigg.\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)\right]

(22)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\Big[\Big.\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;cd}\left({x_{1},x_{2}}\right)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;\alpha\beta}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta;cd}\right](x_{1},x_{2})\Big.\Big]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\}.

The reduced two-loop mass factorization kernel is defined as
Currie:2013vh
:

\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;cd}\left({x_{1},x_{2}}\right)=\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ca,\text{full}}\left({x_{1}}\right)\delta_{db}\delta(1-x_{2})+\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{db,\text{full}}\left({x_{2}}\right)\delta_{ca}\delta(1-x_{1}),

(23)

where
\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ca,\text{full}}\left({x_{i}}\right)
are directly related to the LO and NLO Altarelli-Parisi spitting kernels
Altarelli:1977zs
;
Curci:1980uw
;
Furmanski:1980cm
:

\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab,\text{full}}\left({x}\right)=-\dfrac{1}{2\epsilon}\left(P^{1}_{ab}(x)+\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}P^{0}_{ab}(x)\right),

(24)

and can be decomposed into colour layers as
Currie:2013vh
:

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qq,\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\bigg)\bigg[N_{c}\overline{\Gamma}_{qq}^{(2)}(x)+\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qq}^{(2)}(x)+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\widetilde{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}_{qq}^{(2)}(x)+N_{f}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qq}^{(2)}(x)\bigg],

(25)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{q{\bar{q}},\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\bigg)\bigg[\overline{\Gamma}_{q{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}(x)+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}_{q{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}(x)\bigg],

(26)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qq^{\prime},\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\bigg)\ \overline{\Gamma}_{qq^{\prime}}^{(2)}(x),

(27)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{q{\bar{q}}^{\prime},\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\bigg)\ \overline{\Gamma}_{q{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}^{(2)}(x),

(28)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{gq,\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\bigg)\bigg[N_{c}\overline{\Gamma}_{gq}^{(2)}(x)+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}_{gq}^{(2)}(x)+N_{f}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}_{gq}^{(2)}(x)\bigg],

(29)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qg,\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\overline{\Gamma}_{qg}^{(2)}(x)+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qg}^{(2)}(x)+N_{f}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}_{qg}^{(2)}(x),

(30)

\displaystyle{\overline{\Gamma}}_{gg,\text{full}}^{(2)}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}^{2}\overline{\Gamma}_{gg}^{(2)}(x)+N_{c}N_{f}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}_{gg}^{(2)}(x)+\frac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\widehat{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}_{gg}^{(2)}(x)+N_{f}^{2}\widehat{\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}}_{gg,}^{(2)}(x).

(31)

The expressions for the two-loop mass factorization kernels are rather lengthy and therefore we do not report them here. Their expressions as well as detailed explanations about how they are extracted from the associated LO and NLO splitting kernels
Furmanski:1980cm
can be found in Appendix A.2 of
Currie:2013vh
.

3
Integrated dipoles in colour space

In this section we introduce key ingredients for the colourful antenna subtraction approach:
integrated dipoles

Currie:2013vh
;
Currie:2013dwa
;
Chen:2022ktf
;
Chen:2022tpk
. Such objects conveniently collect integrated antenna functions and mass factorization kernels and are cast as insertion operators in colour space. Integrated dipoles allow to systematically describe the infrared singularity structure of one- and two-loop matrix elements in terms of integrated antenna functions. This is a pivotal prerequisite for the formulation of the colourful antenna subtraction method.

Before illustrating the integrated dipoles, we provide a brief overview of the colour space formalism and of the infrared singularities of virtual amplitudes.

3.1
Colour space formalism

The treatment of QCD amplitudes in colour space provides a universal description of the infrared singularity structure, in the presence of both virtual and real corrections
Bassetto:1983mvz
;
Catani:1996jh
;
Catani:1998bh
;
Catani:1999ss
;
Becher:2009qa
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Gardi:2009zv
.

In colour space, an
\ell
-loop amplitude with
n
external partons is represented by an abstract vector
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}
, whose components carry the colour indices of the external partons in a suitable representation of SU
(N_{c})
. If a set of generating vectors
\left\{\ket{\mathcal{C}_{n,c}^{\ell}}\right\}
is defined, which span the
n
-parton colour space, any amplitude can be decomposed as:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=\sum_{c\in I^{\ell}}A^{\ell}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\,\ket{\mathcal{C}_{n,c}^{\ell}},

(32)

where
I^{\ell}
indicates a suitable subset of generating vectors. The scalar quantities
A^{\ell}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})
are
colour-ordered partial amplitudes
. In equation (
32
), the dependence on the helicities of the external partons is implicit and in the following a sum over helicity configurations is always assumed when squared quantities are considered. For
\ell\geq 1
, the dependence on the renormalization scale
\mu_{r}
is understood.

An arbitrary number of colourless particles, such as photons, weak bosons or Higgs bosons, could be involved in the considered scattering process, however this does not modify the structure of the amplitude in colour space or the construction of the subtraction terms for infrared singularities in QCD. The presence of colourless emissions only affects the colour-ordered partial amplitudes, which clearly change for different processes. For this reason, in the following we restrict the discussion and the notation to
n
-parton amplitudes, modulo additional colourless particles.

Our convention is to strip the partial amplitudes of overall coefficients such as couplings and incoming particles average factors, which are inserted later at the cross section level. In particular, we strip an
\ell
-loop amplitude of an overall factor
\left(\tfrac{\alpha_{s}\bar{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{\ell}
with respect to the corresponding tree-level amplitude, where
\overline{C}(\epsilon)=(4\pi)^{\epsilon}e^{-\epsilon\gamma_{E}}
.

The description of infrared singularities of QCD amplitudes can be achieved through the evaluation of infrared insertion operators in colour space
Catani:1996jh
;
Catani:1998bh
;
Becher:2009qa
;
DelDuca:2016ily
. The action of an operator
\bm{\mathcal{O}}
in colour space can be generically computed as

\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell_{1}}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})|\bm{\mathcal{O}}|\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell_{2}}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}.

(33)

In particular, the coherent emission of a gluon between a dipole formed by parton
i
and parton
j
is described in colour space by the colour charge dipole operator:

\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j},

(34)

where we use bold symbols to emphasize that
\bm{T}_{i}=(T_{i})^{a}_{bc}
is a vector of
a
SU
(N_{c})
generators in the appropriate representation, with
a
indicating the colour index of the emitted gluon. In general, in the remainder of the paper, bold symbols are used to denote operators in colour space, while Roman symbols indicate scalars in colour space. For different partonic species we have:

(T_{i})^{a}_{bc}=\begin{cases}if_{bac}\quad&\text{for }i=\text{ gluon}\\
t^{a}_{bc}\quad&\text{for }i=\text{ final-state quark or initial-state antiquark}\\
(t^{a}_{bc})^{\dagger}=-t^{a}_{cb}\quad&\text{for }i=\text{ final-state antiquark or initial state quark}\\
\end{cases}

(35)

where
f_{abc}
and
t^{a}_{bc}
are the colour-charge matrices in the adjoint and fundamental representations of SU
(N_{c})
. The following properties hold:

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{j}\cdot\bm{T}_{i},

(36)

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{i}^{2}=C_{i}\,\mathbf{Id},

(37)

where
\mathbf{Id}
represents the identity operator in colour space and
C_{i}
indicates the Casimir coefficient for the SU
(N_{c})
representation associated to parton
i
:
C_{g}=C_{A}=N_{c}
and
C_{q}=C_{\bar{q}}=C_{F}=(N_{c}^{2}-1)/(2N_{c})
.

Each state
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}
is a colour singlet and colour conservation implies:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{T}_{i}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\ell}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=0.

(38)

Since in what follows we always consider colour singlet states, we can employ the previous identity as
\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{T}_{j}=-\bm{T}_{i}
.

The explicit evaluation of squared matrix elements or colour operator insertions depends on the form of the generating vectors
\left\{\ket{\mathcal{C}_{n,i}^{\ell}}\right\}
, and, in general, produces numerous terms and non-trivial structures, especially for high-multiplicity processes. Nevertheless one can observe that the result of such calculations is given by real functions of the external momenta and possibly the renormalization scale. At tree-level, the general form of such a function is:

f_{0}\left(\set{p}_{n}\right)=\sum_{c,c^{\prime}\in I^{0}}C^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime})\,a_{n}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n}),

(39)

where we generically assume that the indices
c
and
c^{\prime}
here run over the set of possible colour orderings, along with the possible colour structures. The coefficients
C^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime})
are colour factors which depend on
N_{c}
and the number of partons (possibly depending on flavour) and

a_{n}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n})=\begin{cases}\left|A_{n,c}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\right|^{2}\quad&\text{if }c=c^{\prime},\\
2\text{Re}\left[A_{n,c}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})^{\dagger}A_{n,c^{\prime}}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\right]\quad&\text{if }c\neq c^{\prime}.\end{cases}

(40)

This quantity represents the squared interference of two colour-ordered partial amplitudes, with generic colour structures dictated by the indices
c
and
c^{\prime}
. When
c=c^{\prime}
, we have squared coherent partial amplitudes, while we refer to the case
c\neq c^{\prime}
as
incoherent interference
. In general,
A_{n,c}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})^{\dagger}A_{n,c^{\prime}}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})
is not real. However, in the calculation of any quantity like (
33
), its complex conjugate (obtained by swapping
c
and
c^{\prime}
) always appears with the same prefactor, hence only the real part matters. Analogously, at one loop we have:

f_{1}\left(\set{p}_{n}\right)=\sum_{c\in I^{0},c^{\prime}\in I^{1}}C^{1}_{n}(c,c^{\prime})\,a_{n}^{1}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n}),

(41)

where

a_{n}^{1}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n})=2\text{Re}\left[A_{n,c}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})^{\dagger}A_{n,c^{\prime}}^{1}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\right],

(42)

which is manifestly a real quantity.

3.2
Infrared singularity structure of QCD loop amplitudes

We review here how the infrared singularities of virtual corrections amplitudes in QCD can be predicted in a general way by means of infrared operators in colour space
Catani:1998bh
;
Becher:2009qa
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Gardi:2009zv
.

3.2.1
Infrared singularity structure at one loop

The singularity structure of renormalized
(n+2)
-parton one-loop amplitudes in QCD can be described in colour space with
Catani:1998bh
:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}=\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1,\text{fin}}(\mu_{r}^{2})},

(43)

where
\mu_{r}
is the renormalization scale,
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1,\text{fin}}(\mu_{r}^{2})}
is a finite remainder and
\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)
is Catani’s infrared insertion operator given by
Catani:1998bh
:

\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)=\dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}\dfrac{1}{\bm{T}_{i}^{2}}\mathcal{V}_{i}(\epsilon)\sum_{j\neq i}(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})\left(\dfrac{-s_{ij}}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\,.

(44)

The singular functions
\mathcal{V}_{i}(\epsilon)
contain double and single
\epsilon
-poles:

\mathcal{V}_{i}(\epsilon)=\bm{T}^{2}_{i}\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\gamma_{i}\dfrac{1}{\epsilon},

(45)

with

\displaystyle\bm{T}^{2}_{q}=\bm{T}^{2}_{\bar{q}}=C_{F},\quad\bm{T}^{2}_{g}=C_{A},\quad\gamma_{q}=\gamma_{\bar{q}}=\dfrac{3}{2}C_{F},\quad\gamma_{g}=\dfrac{11}{6}C_{A}-\dfrac{1}{3}N_{f}.

(46)

Equation (
44
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n+2}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

(47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)}\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right),

where in the last line the sum runs over unordered pairs of partons and:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{i_{g}j_{g}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left[\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\dfrac{11}{6}-\dfrac{1}{3}\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\right)\right]\left(\dfrac{-s_{ij}}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon},

(48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{i_{g}j_{q}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)=\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{i_{q}j_{g}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left[\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\dfrac{5}{3}-\dfrac{1}{6}\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\right)\right]\left(\dfrac{-s_{ij}}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon},

(49)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{i_{q}j_{q^{\prime}}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left[\dfrac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\dfrac{3}{2\epsilon}\right]\left(\dfrac{-s_{ij}}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon},

(50)

independently from the flavour of
q
and
q^{\prime}
.
N_{f}
indicates the number of light quark flavours.

Using (
43
) it is possible to extract the poles of one-loop matrix elements in the following way:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\right)

(51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}})\,.

The appearance of the sum
\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)
indicates that only the real part of the insertion operator affects the description of the poles at the matrix element level, as expected. We can then write

\mathcal{P}oles\left(\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{1}\right)=\mathcal{P}oles\left[\sum_{(i,j)}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\,2\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right]\,,

(52)

where we considered that the colour charge evaluation on the tree-level amplitude gives a real quantity, as argued in Section
3
. At the cross section level we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left({\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{NLO}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}\int\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};p_{1},p_{2})\,J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(53)

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{P}oles\left[\sum_{(i,j)}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}2\,\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right],

where
J_{n}^{(m)}(\left\{p\right\}_{m})
represents the jet algorithm, which reconstructs
n
resolved jets from
m
final-state partons with momenta
\left\{p\right\}_{m}
(here
m=n
). The factor
\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}
is given by

\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}=\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\mathcal{N}_{LO},

(54)

where
\mathcal{N}_{LO}
contains the overall factors appropriate for the LO process, such as the strong coupling, symmetry factors and the spin- and colour-average over the initial-state partons.

3.2.2
Infrared singularity structure at two loops

The singularity structure of renormalized two-loop amplitudes in QCD can be described in colour space by
Catani:1998bh
;
Becher:2009qa
;
Gardi:2009qi
;
Gardi:2009zv
:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{2}}=\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}+\bm{I}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{2,\text{fin}}(\mu_{r}^{2})},

(55)

where, as before,
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{2,\text{fin}}(\mu_{r}^{2})}
is a finite remainder. The two-loop infrared insertion operator has the following expression
Catani:1998bh
:

\displaystyle\bm{I}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2};\left\{p\right\}_{n}\right)-\dfrac{1}{2}\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

(56)

\displaystyle+e^{-\epsilon\gamma_{E}}\dfrac{\Gamma(1-2\epsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}+K\right)\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(2\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\bm{H}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\,.

where

\displaystyle\beta_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{11}{6}N_{c}-\dfrac{1}{3}N_{f},

(57)

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{67}{18}-\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{6}\right)N_{c}-\dfrac{5}{9}N_{f}.

(58)

The colour structure of (
56
) is more involved than the colour charge dipole structure of (
44
), with products of two colour charge dipoles appearing. The last line of (
56
) contains the hard radiation function
\bm{H}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

Catani:1998bh
;
Bern:2003ck
;
Becher:2009qa
, which can be decomposed in the following manner:

\bm{H}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)=\sum_{i}C_{i}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{i}\left(\epsilon\right)\mathbf{Id}+\check{\bm{H}}^{(2)}(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}),

(59)

where the sum runs over the
n+2
external partons and
C_{i}
are Casimir coefficients. The first term in (
59
) is proportional to the identity in colour space, while the second term has a non-trivial colour structure, which cannot be in general expressed in terms of colour charge dipoles. However, the second term vanishes when evaluated between tree-level states after summing over helicities
Bern:2003ck
;
Seymour:2008xr
;
Becher:2009qa
;
Czakon:2013hxa
:

\bra{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\check{\bm{H}}^{(2)}(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2})\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}=0.

(60)

For the purpose of describing the infrared singularity structure of two-loop squared matrix elements, the hard radiation function
\bm{H}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)
needs to be evaluated on tree-level states and therefore it is possible to neglect
\check{\bm{H}}^{(2)}(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2})
in its decomposition. We can express a colour operator proportional to the identity as a sum of colour charge dipoles using colour conservation (
38
). We can then rewrite:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}C_{i}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{i}\left(\epsilon\right)\mathbf{Id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{i}\left(\epsilon\right)\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{(i,j)}\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon\right)\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\,,

where, as usual, the sum runs over pairs of partons and
\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{ij}=\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{i}+\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{j}
. The hard radiation functions are given by
Catani:1998bh
;
Becher:2009qa
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{g}\left(\epsilon\right)=\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{4\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\epsilon}N_{c}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{5}{12}+\dfrac{11}{144}\pi^{2}+\dfrac{\zeta_{3}}{2}\right]+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\left[-\dfrac{89}{108}-\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{72}\right]

(62)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}^{3}}\left[\dfrac{-1}{4}\right]+\dfrac{N_{f}^{2}}{N_{c}^{2}}\left[\dfrac{5}{27}\right]\Biggr\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{q}\left(\epsilon\right)=\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{4\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\epsilon}N_{c}\Biggl\{

\displaystyle\left[\dfrac{409}{432}-\dfrac{11}{48}\pi^{2}+\dfrac{7}{2}\zeta_{3}\right]+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\left[-\dfrac{25}{108}+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{24}\right]

(63)

\displaystyle+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}\left[\dfrac{3}{16}-\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}+3\zeta_{3}\right]\Biggr\}\,.

Using (
47
) and (
61
) and neglecting
\check{\bm{H}}^{(2)}(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2})
, we can rearrange equation (
56
) as:

\displaystyle\bm{I}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\sum_{(i,j)}\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}

(64)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{(i,j)}\sum_{(k,l)}\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{kl}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l})

\displaystyle+\sum_{(i,j)}\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j},

where

\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)=e^{-\epsilon\gamma_{E}}\dfrac{\Gamma(1-2\epsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}+K\right)\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(2\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)-\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon\right)\,.

(65)

We can now use (
43
), (
55
) and (
56
) to express the singularity structure of a two-loop matrix element:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{2}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{2}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}\right)

(66)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left(\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\left(\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle+\text{e}^{-\epsilon\gamma_{E}}\dfrac{\Gamma(1-2\epsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}+K\right)\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(2\epsilon\right)+\bm{I}^{(1),\dagger}\left(2\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{H}^{(2)}\left(\epsilon\right)+\bm{H}^{(2),\dagger}\left(\epsilon\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\}\,.

We see again that only the real parts of the insertion operators are needed to describe the singularity structure. Using (
47
) and (
64
) it is possible to recast equation (
66
) as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Big\{\Big.\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\left[\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}\right]

(67)

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{(i,j)}\sum_{(k,l)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{lk}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l})|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\}.

Therefore, the poles of the double-virtual contribution to the cross section are given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left({\hat{\sigma}^{VV}_{NNLO}}\right)=\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int\text{d}\Phi_{n+2}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};p_{1},p_{2})\,J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(68)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Big\{\Big.\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\left[\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}\right]

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{(i,j)}\sum_{(k,l)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{lk}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l})|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{(i,j)}2\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i_{g}}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\},

where

\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}=\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\mathcal{N}_{LO}\,.

(69)

3.3
Integrated dipoles from antenna functions

We are now ready to discuss the construction of integrated dipoles in colour space. We distinguish two types of integrated dipoles: identity-preserving (IP) and identity-changing (IC). The former reproduce the infrared singularity structure of virtual corrections and are naturally cast as operators in colour space. The latter address identity-changing initial-state collinear singularities.

3.3.1
One-loop integrated dipoles

We begin by discussing one-loop integrated dipoles, originally presented in
Currie:2013vh
. They are obtained combining integrated three-parton tree-level antenna functions
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}
and NLO mass factorization kernels
\Gamma_{ab}^{(1)}
.

Identity-preserving dipoles

We define the following one-loop singularity dipole operator in colour space for an
(n+2)
-parton process:

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i,j)\geq 3}(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)+\sum_{i\neq 1,2}(\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{i})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(1,i)

(70)

\displaystyle\,+\sum_{i\neq 1,2}(\bm{T}_{2}\cdot\bm{T}_{i})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(2,i)+(\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{2})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(1,2)\,.

The first sum runs over all pairs of partons in the final state, the second and the third sums include all pairs with an initial-state parton (respectively
1
or
2
) and a final-state one and the last term addresses the configuration where both partons are in the initial state. The scalar functions
\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)
are identity-preserving
colour stripped one-loop integrated dipoles
. These can be classified according to the flavour of the partons
(i,j)
and their kinematical configuration: final-final (FF), initial-final (IF) or initial-initial (II). They can be further decomposed as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,\bar{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{2}^{(1)}(q,\bar{q}),

(71)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{2}^{(1)}(i,g)+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(i,g),\quad i=q,g\,.

(72)

We list the IP quark-antiquark, quark-gluon and gluon-gluon one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles in Tables
1
-
3
. We note that IF and II integrated antenna functions exhibit a dependence on the momentum fractions
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, which is omitted in the previous expressions and in the remainder of the paper for simplicity. We use the following shorthand notation:
\delta_{i}=\delta(1-x_{i})
. We introduced, where needed, dedicated subscripts which accompany the kinematical configuration labels IF and II, to distinguish between different choices of initial-state partons.

Integrated dipoles

FF

J_{2}^{(1)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})=\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})

IF

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}})=\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}(s_{1i})-\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}})=\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}(s_{12})-\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}-\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

Identity-preserving quark-antiquark one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

FF

J_{2}^{(1)}(i_{q},j_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(i_{q},j_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})

IF
q

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},i_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}_{3,q}^{0}(s_{1i})-\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{q},i_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{3,q}^{0}(s_{1i})

IF

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{q})=\mathcal{D}_{3,g,gq}^{0}(s_{1i})-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{q})=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{1}}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{g})=\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{0}(s_{12})-\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{g})=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{2}}\right)\delta_{1}

Table 2:
Identity-preserving quark-gluon one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles. The subscripts indicate different choices of initial-state partons.

Integrated dipoles

FF

J_{2}^{(1)}(i_{g},j_{g})=\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{F}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(i_{g},j_{g})=\mathcal{G}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})

IF

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}_{3,g}^{0}(s_{1i})-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}(s_{1i})-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{1}}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{g},2_{g})=\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}(s_{12})-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{g},2_{g})=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{1}}\right)\delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{2}}\right)\delta_{1}

Table 3:
Identity-preserving gluon-gluon one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

As detailed in the next paragraph, the presence of splitting kernels in association with integrated IF and II antenna functions is related to the mass
factorization of initial-state collinear singularities, which is part of the definition of the integrated dipoles.
One can therefore understand the appearance of certain splitting kernels in a given integrated dipole by considering the possible emissions occurring from the initial-state partons. For example, within the IP dipoles considered above, only
\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}
,
\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}
, and
\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}
can appear, since the emission of a gluon does not change the species of the parton entering the hard process.

Cancellation of infrared singularities at one-loop

The structure of the IP integrated dipoles is chosen in such a way that the singularities carried by the mass factorization kernels cancel with poles in the integrated IF and II antenna functions associated with initial-state collinear divergences. The remaining
\epsilon
-poles exactly match the ones of the virtual matrix element, once the operator in (
70
) is evaluated on the corresponding LO amplitude in colour space. In particular, at one loop the following relation holds:

\mathcal{P}oles\left[\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\right]=\mathcal{P}oles\left[\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{ij}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right],

(73)

where the integrated dipole on the left-hand-side can be in the FF, IF or II configuration. We have explicitly verified that the relation above is satisfied for all the partonic and kinematical configurations. Given that unintegrated antenna functions capture the singular behaviour associated to unresolved emissions between a pair of hard radiators, it is not surprising that we can systematically match the singularity structure of dipole insertion operators onto integrated antenna functions
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
.

Equation (
73
) establishes a correspondence between the operators defined in (
70
) and (
44
), which will be crucial for the formulation of the colourful antenna subtraction method.

Identity-changing dipoles

For the IF and II configurations, we also introduce identity-changing integrated dipoles, obtained from IC antenna functions and splitting kernels. Contrary to the IP ones, they are not defined as operators in colour space, for reasons that will be explained in Section
4
. Their structure can however be inferred from the one of splitting kernels at NLO in (
8
):

\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathcal{J}_{2,a\to c}^{(1)}(x_{1})\delta_{db}\delta(1-x_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{2,b\to d}^{(1)}(x_{2})\delta_{ca}\delta(1-x_{1}).

(74)

We can distinguish between the
quark-to-gluon
and
gluon-to-quark
dipoles, which reflect the decomposition of the splitting kernels associated to the corresponding IC splitting:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(g,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)J_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(g,i),

(75)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(q,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(q,i)\,,

(76)

where the first argument denotes the parton species which enters the hard scattering, while the second argument
i=q,g
is a spectator parton which is not directly involved in the initial-state collinear emission and can be either in the final (IF) or initial (II) state. For identity-changing configurations we need to consider that the spin-averaging factor for a gluon and a quark differ in
d
-dimensions. To properly take this into account and compensate for the mismatch within antenna functions and splitting kernels, we introduce the following factors:

S_{g\to q}=\dfrac{2-2\epsilon}{2}=1-\epsilon,\quad S_{q\to g}=\dfrac{2}{2-2\epsilon}=\dfrac{1}{1-\epsilon}.

(77)

The expressions for the IC quark-antiquark, quark-gluon and gluon-gluon one-loop integrated dipoles are given in Tables
4
–
6
.

Integrated dipoles

IF

J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}})=-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}(s_{1i})-S_{g\to q}\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}})=-\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}(s_{12})-S_{g\to q}\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

Table 4:
Identity-changing quark-antiquark one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles. The subscripts indicate different choices of initial-state partons.

Integrated dipoles

IF
g→q

J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(1_{q},i_{g})=-\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}(s_{1i})-S_{g\to q}\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

IF
q→g

J_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{q})=-\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}}^{0}(s_{1i})-S_{q\to g}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II
g→q

J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{g})=-\mathcal{D}_{3,gg}^{0}(s_{12})-S_{g\to q}\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II
q→g

J_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(1_{g},2_{q})=-\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{0}(s_{12})-S_{q\to g}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

IF

J_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(1_{g},i_{g})=-\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}(s_{1i})-S_{q\to g}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2,q\to g}^{(1)}(1_{g},2_{g})=-\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}(s_{12})-S_{q\to g}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

Table 6:
Identity-changing gluon-gluon one-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

As expected, we see the appearance of IC splitting kernels
\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}
and
\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}
, associated to a final-quark becoming collinear to an initial-state parton.

The IC integrated dipoles are free of explicit infrared singularities:

\mathcal{P}oles\left[\mathcal{J}_{2,a\to b}^{(1)}(i,j)\right]=0,

(78)

because the only
\epsilon
-poles which are present in integrated IC antenna functions have an initial-state collinear origin and completely cancel against the analogous ones in the IC splitting kernels.

3.3.2
Two-loop integrated dipoles

The characteristic new ingredients of two-loop integrated dipoles are integrated four-parton tree-level antenna functions
\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}
, three-parton one-loop antenna functions
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}
, convolutions of two three-parton tree-level antenna functions
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}
and two-loop mass factorization kernels
\overline{\Gamma}_{ab}^{(2)}
. Analogously to the one-loop case, the presence of certain two-loop splitting kernels in association with integrated antenna functions can be understood considering the possible emissions from initial-state partons.

Also at two-loop we distinguish between identity-preserving and identity-changing integrated dipoles, however a significant difference with respect to the one-loop case has to be highlighted. Explicit integrated results are available at NLO, not only for all the three-parton tree-level antenna functions, but also for any sub-antenna, given the simplicity of the analytical integration. This means that both at the unintegrated and integrated level one has complete control over which infrared-divergent configurations are considered. This is not true at NNLO, where the unavailability of individual integrated sub-antennae forces one to include additional infrared singularities within the identity-preserving two-loop integrated dipoles. These singularities have to be properly identified and subtracted. This is achieved by introducing spurious terms which target the undesired structures. We identify three classes of such terms:

•

flip-flopping contributions:
address IC initial-state collinear limits like
g\to q\to g
or
q\to g\to q
, where the identity of the initial-state emitter formally coincides with the original one. These contributions are denoted in the following by the subscript
\mathrm{f/f}
;

•

IC corrective contributions:
target genuine IC limits which need to be removed from IP structures, denoted with the subscript
\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}.
;

•

unphysical triple-collinear limits:
limits existing in the supersymmetric matrix elements used to extract antenna functions, which are not present in QCD. They are treated introducing suitable insertion operators.

We will comment further about each class in the paragraphs below. Expressions for the two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles used for the construction of NNLO subtraction terms for di-jet production can be found in
Chen:2022tpk
. In the following we review them and discuss in more detail how to generalize their construction for any process. To simplify the expressions, we omit the scale
s_{ij}
dependence of the integrated antenna functions.

We mention that the complete development of designer antenna functions
Braun-White:2023sgd
;
Braun-White:2023zwd
at NNLO will bring significant simplifications to some of the structures presented below. Indeed, the assemblage of antenna functions from specific infrared limits, rather than from physical matrix elements, naturally prevents the introduction of spurious divergences and therefore the necessity of the corrective terms introduced above.

Identity-preserving dipoles

In analogy with (
70
), we can define a two-loop insertion operator in colour space:

\displaystyle\mathbfcal{J}^{(2)}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=N_{c}\sum_{(i,j)\geq 3}(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(i_{g},j_{g})+N_{c}\sum_{i\neq 1,2}(\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{i})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(\hat{1}_{g},i_{g})

(79)

\displaystyle\,+N_{c}\sum_{i\neq 1,2}(\bm{T}_{2}\cdot\bm{T}_{i})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(\hat{2}_{g},i_{g})+N_{c}\,(\bm{T}_{1}\cdot\bm{T}_{2})\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(\hat{1}_{g},\hat{2}_{g})\,.

where the functions accompanying the charge dipoles are identity-preserving
two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles
. They can be decomposed according to:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}\left(q,\bar{q}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{2}^{(2)}\left(q,\bar{q}\right)-\dfrac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(q,\bar{q}\right)+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(q,\bar{q}\right)

(80)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}\left(g,i\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{2}^{(2)}\left(g,i\right)+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\,\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(g,i\right)

(81)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}^{3}}\hat{\tilde{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(g,i\right)+\dfrac{N_{f}^{2}}{N_{c}^{2}}\,\hat{\hat{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(g,i\right),\quad i=g,q\,.

Quark-antiquark integrated dipoles

The IP two-loop quark-antiquark colour-stripped integrated dipoles can be constructed in a straightforward manner, since the quark-antiquark NNLO antenna functions specifically contain only the desired physical limits in any kinematical configuration and allow for a unique identification of the
hard radiator partons. Even if sub-antennae need to be employed, for example for
\widetilde{A}_{4}^{0}

Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
, this is only required to properly define a colour-ordered momentum mapping. Therefore, for a given hard quark-antiquark dipole, no spurious structure is present in the associated integrated antenna functions. The IP quark-antiquark two-loop integrated dipoles are listed in Table
7
.

Integrated dipoles

FF

J_{2}^{(2)}\left(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{4}^{0}+\mathcal{A}_{3}^{1}+\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{ij}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\right]

\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{0}+2\mathcal{C}_{4}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\right]

\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{B}_{4}^{0}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{3}^{1}+\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{ij}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}

IF

J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{4,q}^{0}+\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{1}+\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}}\right)}-\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{qq}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}

\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,q}^{0}+2\mathcal{C}_{4,q}^{0}+\mathcal{C}_{4,\bar{q}}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,q}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\right]

\phantom{\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}}\right)}+\widetilde{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{B}_{4,q}^{0}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{3,q}^{1}+\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}-\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{4,q\bar{q}}^{0}+\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{1}+\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)}-\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{qq}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}-\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{qq}\left({2}\right)\delta_{1}

\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,q\bar{q}}^{0}+2\mathcal{C}_{4,q\bar{q}}^{0}+2\mathcal{C}_{4,\bar{q}q}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q\bar{q}}^{0}\right]

\phantom{\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)}+\widetilde{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}+\widetilde{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)=\mathcal{B}_{4,q{\bar{q}}}^{0}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{3,qq}^{1}+\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{A}_{3,qq}^{0}

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}}\right)}-\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}-\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

Table 7:
Identity-preserving quark-antiquark two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

Quark-gluon integrated dipoles

The IP quark-gluon dipoles are particularly affected by the presence of undesired singularities. This is mostly due to quark-gluon NNLO antenna functions containing any allowed unresolved limit among the involved partons, with no possibility to clearly disentangle the hard radiators from the unresolved partons
at the integrated level. The IP quark-gluon two-loop colour stripped integrated dipoles are given in Table
8
.

Integrated dipoles

\phantom{J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)}-\left[\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{gg}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}+J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}.}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})

\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)=\mathcal{E}_{4,g}^{0}+\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{3,g}^{1}+\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}+\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)+\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}.}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})

\hat{\tilde{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{4,g}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}+\hat{\tilde{J}}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)

\hat{\hat{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{g}\right)=\mathcal{D}_{4,qg}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{D}_{4,qg^{\prime}}^{0}+\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{1}+\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{0}

\phantom{J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{g}\right)}-\left[\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{0}\right]-\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}

\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{g}\right)=\mathcal{E}_{4,qg}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{3,qg}^{1}+\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{D}_{3,qg}^{0}-\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{qq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{g}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}+\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q}2_{g}\right)

\hat{\tilde{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{4,qg}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta_{1}+\hat{\tilde{J}}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q}2_{g}\right)

Table 8:
Identity-preserving quark-gluon two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles. The subscripts indicate different choices of initial-state partons.

As anticipated, we see the appearance of corrective contributions. The flip-flopping terms remove triple-collinear limits
g\parallel q^{\prime}\parallel{\bar{q}}^{\prime}
with the gluon in the initial state, which are present in the
\mathcal{E}_{4,g}^{0}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{4,g}^{0}
,
\mathcal{E}_{4,qg}^{0}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{4,qg}^{0}
integrated antenna functions. Technically, the identity of the hard initial-state parton after integration is preserved, but the infrared divergences associated to such an unresolved configuration are absorbed by IC spitting kernels, rather than by virtual corrections. Therefore, in the definition of genuinely IP integrated dipoles, these singularities need to be removed. The flip-flopping terms are given in Table
9
.

Integrated dipoles

IF

\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)=S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]

\hat{\tilde{J}}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{q}\right)=S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]

II

\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{q}\right)=S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,qq^{\prime}}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{2})\right]

\hat{\tilde{J}}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{q}\right)=S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,qq^{\prime}}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{2})\right]

Table 9:
Flip-flopping contributions to identity-preserving quark-gluon two-loop integrated dipoles.

Analogously, spurious IC singularities are present in the
\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}
,
\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{1}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{3,g}^{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{4,g}^{0}
antenna functions. In particular, these gluon-initiated antenna functions contain
\epsilon
-poles due to the final-state quark becoming collinear to the initial-state gluon. This is an IC unresolved limit, which cannot be isolated from the remaining IP ones due to the unavailability of dedicated integrated sub-antennae. It is possible to remove such singularities by a suitable combination of gluon-initiated IC quark-gluon and quark-antiquark antenna functions. The former have the same gluon and quark involved in the dipole as hard radiators. The latter have one quark (antiquark) coinciding with the one in the quark-gluon dipole, and so colour-connected to the initial-state gluon, while the other antiquark (quark) is its colour-connected partner. We note that in such quark-antiquark antennae, no divergent behaviour is associated with the antiquark (quark) becoming collinear to the initial-state gluon. If the antiquark (quark) is in the final state, the introduced antenna functions will be in the initial-final configurations, otherwise, if it is in the initial state, the antenna functions will be in the initial-initial configuration. The explicit expressions according to the kinematical configurations of the quark-antiquark pair are given in Table
10
.

Integrated dipoles

IF

J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)=-\left[\mathcal{D}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+2\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\mathcal{A}_{4,g}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,g}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{1}

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,g}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}-1\right)\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}+\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}\right]-\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}-\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\right)

\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)=-\left[\mathcal{E}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]

\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{3,g}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}-1\right)\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}

\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}\right]-\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}-\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\right)

II

J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)=-\left[\mathcal{D}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+2\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\mathcal{A}_{4,qg}^{0}-\mathcal{A}_{4,qg^{\prime}}^{0}

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,qg}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{A}_{3,qg}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,qg}^{1}

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}-1\right)\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}+2\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}\right]-2\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}\right]

\phantom{J_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}-\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}-\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\right)

\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)=-\left[\mathcal{E}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]-\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to q}^{0}\right]
