\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{IC}\,\mathrm{corr}}^{(2)}\left(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}}\right)}+\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,gq}^{0}\right]-\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{D}_{3,g}^{0}-\mathcal{D}_{3,g\to g}^{0}\right)

Table 10:
Corrective terms required to remove spurious identity-changing singularities from integrated identity-preserving gluon-initiated quark-gluon antenna functions.

These corrective terms display a quite involved structure. Indeed, even if the presence of spurious singularities within integrated antenna functions is well understood and formally under control, they inevitably complicate the structure of IP integrated dipoles as well as of the subtraction terms. In particular, such corrections propagate into all the layers of the subtraction infrastructure and require careful treatment in any practical implementation.

Finally, the
D
-type tree-level four-parton antenna functions
D_{4}^{0}(q,g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
(FF, IF or II) present a triple-collinear
g_{3}\parallel q\parallel g_{1}
limit which is an unphysical remnant due to the cyclicity of the super-symmetric matrix element used to extract the antenna functions
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005svg
. This limit shows up at the integrated level as spurious
\epsilon
-poles which need to be removed from the integrated dipoles. The collinear poles in a quark-gluon dipole are described by the hard radiation function
\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{g}\left(\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{q}\left(\epsilon\right)
, with
\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{g}\left(\epsilon\right)
and
\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{q}\left(\epsilon\right)
given in (
62
) and (
63
). On the contrary, for the decay of a neutralino to a gluon and a gluino
\chi\to g\tilde{g}
, the process used to extract the antenna functions, the collinear poles are described by
\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{g\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon\right)=\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{g}\left(\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon\right)
, with the quark hard radiation function replace by the gluino one
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005svg
;
Chen:2023egx
:

\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon\right)=\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}{4\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\epsilon}N_{c}\left\{\left[-\dfrac{187}{216}+\dfrac{13}{48}\pi^{2}-\dfrac{1}{2}\zeta_{3}\right]+\dfrac{N_{f}}{N_{c}}\left[-\dfrac{25}{108}+\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{24}\right]\right\}.

(82)

The unphysical poles which are not present in QCD matrix elements are identified by the mismatch between the
N_{f}=0
components of the quark and the gluino hard radiation functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{q}\left(\epsilon\right)-\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{e^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}N_{c}}{4\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\epsilon}\Biggl\{\left[\dfrac{29}{16}-\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{2}+4\zeta_{3}\right]+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}^{2}}\left[\dfrac{3}{16}-\dfrac{\pi^{2}}{4}+3\zeta_{3}\right]\Biggr\}.

(83)

To compensate for the discrepancy above we introduce an auxiliary quark-antiquark two-loop integrated dipole:

\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})=\dfrac{1}{2}\bar{J_{2}}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})+\dfrac{1}{2N_{c}^{2}}\tilde{\bar{J_{2}}}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}}),

(84)

which satisfies:

\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})=\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{q}\left(\epsilon\right)\Bigg|_{N_{f}=0}-\mathcal{H}^{(2)}_{\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon\right)\Bigg|_{N_{f}=0}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0}).

(85)

The integrated dipoles in (
84
) can be written in terms of integrated antenna functions and are listed in Table
11
.

Integrated dipoles

FF

\bar{J_{2}}^{(2)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3}^{0}\right]

\tilde{\bar{J_{2}}}^{(2)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})=\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})

IF

\bar{J_{2}}^{(2)}(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}})=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,q}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,q}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q}^{0}\right]

\tilde{\bar{J_{2}}}^{(2)}(1_{q},i_{\bar{q}})=\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})

II

\bar{J_{2}}^{(2)}(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}})=\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,q{\bar{q}}}^{0}+\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{3,q{\bar{q}}}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{A}_{3,q{\bar{q}}}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,q{\bar{q}}}^{0}\right]

\tilde{\bar{J_{2}}}^{(2)}(1_{q},2_{\bar{q}})=\tilde{J_{2}}^{(2)}(i_{q},j_{\bar{q}})

Table 11:
Auxiliary quark-antiquark two-loop integrated dipoles needed to remove spurious poles present in integrated quark-gluon antenna functions, which are not present in physical matrix elements.

The combination of antenna functions which gives the auxiliary dipoles was found previously in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
and in
Currie:2013vh
during the construction of double-real subtraction terms for
e^{+}e^{-}\to 3j
and
pp\to jj
, as a correction to remove the unphysical triple-collinear limits of the
D
-type antenna functions. The presence of such a combination can be easily tracked down due to the anomalous appearance of subleading-colour quark-antiquark antenna functions within the leading-colour contribution to the subtraction terms.

The newly introduced
\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})
can be promoted to an operator in colour space, however its structure depends on the number of fermionic lines present in the process. For a process involving a single quark-antiquark pair
(q,{\bar{q}})
and an arbitrary number of gluons, we have:

\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}=N_{c}\sum_{g}\left(\bm{T}_{q}+\bm{T}_{\bar{q}}\right)\bm{T}_{g}\,\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}}),

(86)

which has the peculiarity of combining quark-gluon dipole charge operators with quark-antiquark integrated dipoles. This is once again due to quark-gluon integrated antenna functions not exactly reproducing the QCD radiation among the physical quark-gluon dipole.

For processes involving more than one quark-antiquark pair, the appropriate structure in colour space is more complicated. This is explained by the non-trivial colour connections between quarks belonging to different quark-antiquark pairs. The extension of (
84
) to multiple fermionic lines is first required addressing NNLO calculations with
5
partons at Born-level, such as
pp\to 3j
, for the sub-processes with two fermionic lines and one gluon:
q{\bar{q}}q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}g
. Configurations with two fermionic lines and more than one gluon or three fermionic lines with at least one gluon would appear, for example, in the computation of the NNLO correction to
pp\to 4j
and
pp\to 5j
, respectively. Since at present such calculations are well beyond the reach of available two-loop matrix elements and, arguably, computational techniques, we focus on the five-parton configuration. The appropriate colour space structure for two quark-antiquark pairs (possibly of the same flavour), denoted with
(q,{\bar{q}})
and
(q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime})
and a gluon
g
is then:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\left(\bm{T}_{q}+\bm{T}_{\bar{q}}\right)\bm{T}_{g}\,\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})+N_{c}\left(\bm{T}_{q^{\prime}}+\bm{T}_{{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}\right)\bm{T}_{g}\,\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime})

(87)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle N_{c}\left(\bm{T}_{q}-\bm{T}_{\bar{q}}\right)\bm{T}_{g}\,\left(\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}}^{\prime})-\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q^{\prime},{\bar{q}})\right)(\delta_{q,1}+\delta_{q,2})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle N_{c}\left(\bm{T}_{q^{\prime}}-\bm{T}_{{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}\right)\bm{T}_{g}\,\left(\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}}^{\prime})-\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q^{\prime},{\bar{q}})\right)(\delta_{q^{\prime},1}+\delta_{q^{\prime},2}),

where the two last lines are only present if
q
or
q^{\prime}
are in the initial state. We note that the structure above is in principle not unique and other arrangements can correctly reproduce the same singularity structure, thanks to relations among the
\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}
integrated dipoles, such as:

\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(1,2)=\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(1,i)+\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(2,j)-\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(i,j),\quad i,j\geq 3,

(88)

and colour conservation

\bm{T}_{i}=-\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{T}_{j}.

(89)

The configuration of antenna functions in (
87
), in their unintegrated form, correctly subtracts the singular behaviour also at the real-virtual and double-real levels, in infrared limits involving a
q\parallel g
collinear pair.

Gluon-gluon integrated dipoles

The IP gluon-gluon dipoles only require minimal adjustments to the natural arrangement of integrated antenna functions. They are given in Table
12
.

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(i_{g},j_{g}\right)}-\frac{2}{3}\left[\mathcal{G}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{3}^{0}\right]

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{3,g}^{0}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\phantom{\hat{J_{2}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}+\hat{J}_{2,\mathrm{f/f}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)

\hat{\hat{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{3,g}^{1}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}-\frac{1}{4}\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}\right]

\phantom{\hat{\hat{J_{2}}}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}\widehat{\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II

J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)=\mathcal{F}_{4,gg}^{0}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}_{4,gg^{\prime}}^{0}+\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{1}+\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}

\phantom{J_{2}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-\left[\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{gg}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{gg}\left({2}\right)\delta_{1}

Cancellation of infrared singularities at two-loop

In analogy with (
73
), the
\epsilon
-poles of IP two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles can be related to the to the infrared singularities of two-loop matrix elements: any pole of initial-state collinear origin cancel against two-loop mass factorization kernels, and the remaining singularities satisfy:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left[N_{c}\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right]=

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\mathcal{P}oles\left[\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{q{\bar{q}}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{q{\bar{q}}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right],

(90)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left[N_{c}\,\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(g,g)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(g,g)\right]=

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\mathcal{P}oles\left[\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{gg}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{gg}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right],

(91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Bigg[\Bigg.N_{c}\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(q,g)+\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(2)}(g,{\bar{q}})-2\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,g)+\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(g,{\bar{q}})\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left[\text{Re}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{qg}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{I}^{(2)}_{g{\bar{q}}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{gg}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)+\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{g{\bar{q}}}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)\right)\right].

(92)

For quark-gluon dipoles we observe in the last equation the presence of
\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{(2)}
to compensate for unphysical poles. We have explicitly verified that the relations above are satisfied for all the partonic and kinematical configurations.

Initial-initial identity-changing quark-antiquark two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

Identity-changing dipoles

Identity-changing integrated dipoles, as for the one-loop case, are not treated in colour space. We have two possible structures, one is associated to configurations where at least one initial state parton actually changes identity, while the other addresses flip-flopping contributions for which, after integration, the species of the initial-state partons is formally the same as before. The first type reads:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,a\to c}^{(2)}(x_{1})\delta_{db}\delta(1-x_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{2,b\to d}^{(2)}(x_{2})\delta_{ca}\delta(1-x_{1})

(93)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,ab\to cd}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2}),

where either a single initial-state leg changes identity (first two terms) or both legs change identity (last term). The flip-flopping integrated dipoles are given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd;ab}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,a\to c\to a}^{(2)}(x_{1})\delta_{db}\delta(1-x_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{2,b\to d\to b}^{(2)}(x_{2})\delta_{ca}\delta(1-x_{1}).

(94)

The integrated dipoles in (
93
) and (
94
) have a colour decomposition which mirrors the one of two-loop IC splitting kernels:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(g,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)\Big[\Big.N_{c}J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(g,i)+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}}\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(g,i)

(95)

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt+N_{f}\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(g,i)\Big.\Big]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}J_{2,g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}}\tilde{J}_{2,g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)+N_{f}\hat{J}_{2,g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)\,,

(96)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q\to{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}({\bar{q}},i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)\left[J_{2,q\to{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}({\bar{q}},i)+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}}\tilde{J}_{2,q\to{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}({\bar{q}},i)\right]\,,

(97)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q\to q^{\prime}}^{(2)}(q^{\prime},i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)J_{2,q\to q^{\prime}}^{(2)}(q^{\prime},i)\,,

(98)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,gg\to q{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,J_{2,gg\to q{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}}\tilde{J}_{2,gg\to q{\bar{q}}}^{(2)}(q,{\bar{q}})\,,

(99)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,qq^{\prime}\to gg}^{(2)}(g,g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)J_{2,q{\bar{q}}\to gg}^{(2)}(g,g)\,,

(100)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(g,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(g,q)+\dfrac{1}{N_{c}}\tilde{J}_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(g,q)\,,

(101)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,q\to g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{N_{c}^{2}-1}{N_{c}}\right)J_{2,q\to g\to q}^{(2)}(q,i)\,.

(102)

where
i=q,g
. For configurations where only one initial-state parton changes identity, the second hard parton in the dipole acts as a mere spectator and no unresolved limits are associated to it. This means that in any practical implementation one has the freedom to choose different spectators for the same IC limits. In particular, in any IC unresolved configuration resulting in a hard quark, such as
g\to q
,
q\to q^{\prime}
,
q\to{\bar{q}}
and
q\to g\to q
, it is always possible to choose the fermionic partner of the hard quark as a spectator. For this reason, there is no need to define IC integrated dipoles for these configurations with a hard gluon as spectator. In the following we present the expressions for the two-loop IC integrated dipoles. We note that typically for a specific process only some of the listed dipoles are actually needed. However, we provide expressions for all of them for the sake of generality. The IC quark-antiquark, quark-gluon and gluon-gluon two-loop integrated dipoles are listed in Tables
14
–
17
.

In the
J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{q})
and in the
J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})
integrated dipoles of Table
16
, corrective terms had to be included, as one can notice from the appearance of quark-antiquark integrated antenna functions. The origin of such terms is completely analogous to the ones of Table
10
: they are needed to remove the triple collinear
q^{\prime}\parallel{\bar{q}}^{\prime}\parallel q
limit present in the integrated E-type four-parton antenna functions. Indeed, such contributions yields a
q\to g\to q
identity-changing limit which is not compatible with the considered configurations.

\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})=-\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{1}-\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{0}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{q})}-\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{q})}-\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]-S_{q\to g}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})=-\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{4,q^{\prime}q}^{0}-\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{1}+\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})}+\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]+S_{q\to g}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II
{}_{q^{\prime}g\to gq}

J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})=2\mathcal{E}_{4,{\bar{q}}^{\prime}g}^{0}+2S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{D}_{3,gg}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})}+2S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}q}^{0}\right]+\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}\left(x_{2}\right)

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})}-\mathcal{B}_{4,q^{\prime}}^{0}+\left[\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{3,g}^{0}\right]+2S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{E}_{3,q^{\prime}}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q^{\prime}g\to gq}^{(2)}(1_{g},2_{q})}+2\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]

Table 16:
Identity-changing quark-gluon two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

Integrated dipoles

IF

J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)=-\mathcal{G}_{4,q}^{0}-\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{1}-\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{1i}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}+\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{3,g}^{0}\right]-S_{q\to g}\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{gq}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}+\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}\right]-\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{g})}+S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,g}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\otimes\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}\left({x_{1}}\right)\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{g})}-S_{q\to g}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{4,q}^{0}-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{3,q}^{1}+\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,q}^{0}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},i_{g}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]+S_{q\to g}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II
q→g

J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)=-\mathcal{G}_{4,qg}^{0}-\mathcal{G}_{4,qg^{\prime}}^{0}-\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{1}-\frac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}+2\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}\right]-S_{q\to g}\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{gq}\left({1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,qq}^{0}\right]+\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\right]+\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]

\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)=-\widehat{\mathcal{G}}_{3,qg}^{1}-\frac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{|s_{12}|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}-S_{q\to g}\widehat{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\hat{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\otimes\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\right]-\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\otimes\widehat{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{gg}(x_{1})\right]

\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)=-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{4,qg}^{0}-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{3,qg}^{1}+\left[\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}-S_{g\to q}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qg}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,qq}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{\tilde{J}_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{qq}(x_{1})\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\right]+S_{q\to g}\widetilde{\overline{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta_{2}

II
{}_{q{\bar{q}}\to gg}

J_{2,q{\bar{q}}\to gg}^{(2)}\left(1_{g},2_{g}\right)=\mathcal{H}_{4,qq^{\prime}}^{0}+S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{2})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,qg}^{0}\right]

\hskip-28.45274pt\phantom{J_{2,q\to g}^{(2)}(1_{g},i_{q})}+S_{q\to g}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}(x_{1})\otimes\mathcal{G}_{3,gq}^{0}\right]+S_{q\to g}^{2}\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{1}\right)\Gamma^{(1)}_{gq}\left(x_{2}\right)

Table 17:
Identity-changing gluon-gluon two-loop colour-stripped integrated dipoles.

Contrary to the one-loop IC dipoles, two-loop ones are not free from singularities. This is understood considering that, with two unresolved partons, along with initial-state collinear singularities which cancel against the mass factorization kernels, additional soft or final-state collinear poles can be present. As we will see in more detail in Section
5
, the singularities of the IC two-loop integrated dipoles cancel against explicit poles in other structures present in the two-loop mass factorization counterterm for identity-changing splittings. This cancellation yields an IC double-virtual subtraction term which is correctly free from explicit singularities.

4
Colourful antenna subtraction at NLO

In the following, we describe the colourful antenna subtraction approach at NLO, aiming to introduce the underlying concepts and preparing for the
full NNLO formulation.

The NLO correction to a partonic sub-process initiated by partons
a
and
b
reads as follow.

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=\int_{n}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right]+\int_{n+1}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right],

(103)

with

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}.

(104)

Typically, the construction of the subtraction terms would begin by analysing the divergent behaviour of the real emission correction and by individuating the proper combination of antenna functions and reduced LO squared amplitudes which reproduces the implicit infrared singularities. Hence, the real subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
, up to an overall process-dependent normalisation, has the following form
Currie:2013vh
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{i,j,k}\text{d}\Phi_{n+1}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+3};p_{1},p_{2})\,

(105)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\,X_{3}^{0}(i,k,j)\,a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,\widetilde{ik},.,\widetilde{kj},.\})J_{n}^{(n)}(.,p_{\widetilde{ik}},.,p_{\widetilde{kj}},.).

In the notation of Section
3
,
a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime},\{\dots\})
indicates a generic
(n+2)
-parton interference between colour ordering
c
and
c^{\prime}
. The first sum runs over the possible colour structures and orderings, while the inner sum covers permutations of external momenta. In (
105
), as well as analogous expressions in the remainder of the paper, each term in the sum can come with a different numerical factor in front of it. Such factors are not generalizable and not particularly relevant for the description of the structure of the subtraction terms. The symbol
\sim
indicates their omissions.

An analytical integration of (
105
) over the phase space of the unresolved radiation (antenna phase space) yields:

\displaystyle\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{i,j,k}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(.,p_{\widetilde{ik}},.,p_{\widetilde{kj}},.;p_{1},p_{2})\,

(106)

\displaystyle\hskip 31.2982pt\times\,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}(s_{(\widetilde{ik})(\widetilde{kj})})\,a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,\widetilde{ik},.,\widetilde{kj},.\})J_{n}^{(n)}(.,p_{\widetilde{ik}},.,p_{\widetilde{kj}},.)\,,

where the indices
\widetilde{ik}
and
\widetilde{jk}
are the mapped momenta that also feature in the unintegrated subtraction term (
105
).
Finally, the full virtual subtraction term (
6
) is obtained by adding all mass factorization counterterms appropriate for the incoming partons.

In the context of the antenna subtraction method, the construction of real-emission subtraction terms for a generic process may become cumbersome and require a considerable amount of process-specific work. In particular, beyond leading-colour, one cannot directly rely on colour connections of squared amplitudes as the guiding principle for the construction of the subtraction terms, especially at high multiplicities.
The colourful antenna subtraction approach is designed to systematise the construction of the real-emission subtraction term. The main idea behind it consists of exploiting the predictability of the singularity structure of virtual amplitudes in colour space to straightforwardly construct the virtual subtraction term in a completely general way. Subsequently, the real subtraction term is derived with a systematic procedure exploiting the correspondence between integrated and unintegrated antenna functions. To properly introduce how this is done we first need to recast the NLO mass factorization counterterm in colour space.

4.1
NLO mass factorization in colour space

We notice that equation (
7
) has both identity-preserving and identity-changing contributions, respectively when
(c,d)=(a,b)
and
(c,d)\neq(a,b)
. For a given LO partonic matrix element, initiated by partons
a
and
b
, the virtual infrared singularities in (
53
) and the identity-preserving contributions in (
7
) factorize upon the same set of LO partonic colour correlations. Nevertheless, it is possible to express the identity-preserving mass factorization kernels in colour space, in analogy to the singularity structure of one-loop amplitudes. We define:

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)=\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{aa,\text{full}}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta(1-x_{2})+\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{bb,\text{full}}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta(1-x_{1}),

(107)

where, as usual, bold symbols are used to indicate that the splitting kernels have been promoted to be operators in colour space:

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{cc,\text{full}}\left(x_{i}\right)=-\Gamma^{(1)}_{cc,\text{full}}\left(x_{i}\right)\dfrac{1}{C_{c}}\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j},\quad c=a(b),\quad i=1(2).

(108)

This colour operator is proportional to the identity in colour space when it acts on a colour-singlet vector, due to colour conservation:

\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{\ell}}=\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{\ell}},

(109)

which restores the original result. We can then rewrite the identity-preserving (IP) sector of the mass factorization counterterms in (
7
) as:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\int\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})\,J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(110)

\displaystyle\times\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\,,

which is analogous to (
53
).

While it is possible to apply a similar treatment for the identity-changing mass factorization kernels in principle, it is not particularly convenient to rewrite them as operators in colour space. Hence, we choose to maintain the identity-changing mass factorization counterterm as colour scalars:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{(c,d)\neq(a,b)}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\,.

(111)

4.2
NLO virtual subtraction term

The virtual subtraction term at NLO,
{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
can be separated into IP and IC components:

{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}={\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}+{\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\,.

(112)

The IP component has to:

•

remove the explicit poles of the virtual matrix element;

•

contain the IP mass factorization counterterm
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
.

We know that the first requirement can be addressed with (
53
), while
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
is given above in (
110
). By (
70
), (
73
) and the explicit formulae given in Section
3.3.1
.
One can see that the one-loop integrated dipoles that were introduced in Section
3.3.1
address both tasks, yielding the
construction of the NLO IP virtual subtraction term as

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(113)

\displaystyle\times 2\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\,.

The equation above explains the important role of the integrated dipoles and justifies their definition. First of all, we can describe in a general way the singularity structure of one-loop matrix elements, naturally including also the IP mass factorization contribution. Moreover, we achieved this by means of integrated antenna functions, which will be crucial for the next step. The explicit form of (
113
) is obtained computing
\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}
and dressing them with the associated colour stripped integrated dipoles. We remark that (
113
) is a completely process-independent result, which is valid for any multiplicity and retains the full
N_{c}
dependence.

The IC component of the virtual subtraction term is analogously obtained exploiting IC one-loop integrated dipoles. These were defined as scalars in colour space in (
74
), since IC infrared singularities factorize onto full LO squared matrix elements. Hence,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
is given by:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{(c,d)\neq(a,b)}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\,.

(114)

The contribution above contains integrated initial-state collinear limits and the associated mass factorization kernels.

4.3
NLO real subtraction term

In the colourful antenna approach, we aim for a systematic generation of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
, starting from the

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
derived above.
Each term in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
must have an unintegrated counterpart in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
. Since
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
is completely written in terms of integrated antenna functions, there is a one-to-one relation with their unintegrated counterparts:

\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})\,a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime},\{.,i,.,j,.\})\,\leftrightarrow\,X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime},\{.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.\}),

(115)

where
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})
is the integrated antenna function obtained by integrating the tree-level three-parton antenna function
X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)
over the phase space of the unresolved parton
u
. Due to this correspondence, once the virtual subtraction term is obtained, the structure of the real subtraction term can be determined by inserting an unresolved emission between each pair of hard radiators appearing in the integrated dipoles. This involves a transition from an integrated NLO antenna function to an unintegrated one and from a genuine LO colour interference to a
reduced
one where the
{(n+2)}
-particle momenta are meant to be obtained from a
(n+3)
-particle phase space through a suitable mapping, dictated by the accompanying antenna function. The right-hand-side of (
115
) reproduces the divergent behaviour of the real interference
a_{n+3}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,i,.,u,.,j,.\})
when parton
u
is unresolved between the hard pair
(i,j)
.

We will illustrate how the transition between integrated and unintegrated quantities is performed. The procedure can be summarized as follows:

1)

remove the mass-factorization kernels from the colour-stripped integrated dipoles
J_{2}^{(1)}
in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
;

2)

replace each integrated antenna function
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}(s_{ij})
with its unintegrated counterpart
X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)
(see below);

3)

suitably replace the momenta in the colour interferences, according to the accompanying integrated antenna, following (
115
);

4)

apply the same momenta relabelling to the jet function;

5)

promote the phase-space measure to the appropriate one for
(n+1)
final-state momenta;

6)

replace the overall factor with an appropriate one for the real correction:

\mathcal{N}^{V}_{NLO}\to\mathcal{N}^{R}_{NLO}=s_{R}\left(4\pi\alpha_{s}\right)\mathcal{N}_{LO},

(116)

where
s_{R}
compensates the potentially different final-state symmetry factors in the presence of an extra emission;

All the steps above consist of rather simple manipulations, except for step
2
. The replacement of an integrated antenna function with its unintegrated counterpart stands at the core of the whole algorithm and dictates how to perform the subsequent steps. As indicated in (
115
), the transition occurs by inserting an unresolved parton within the hard dipole described by the integrated antenna function. We have to distinguish three different types of insertions:

•

insertion of an unresolved gluon;

•

insertion of an unresolved quark-antiquark pair, coming from a hard gluon splitting;

•

insertion of unresolved partons in IC limits.

These three cases are described in detail in the following.

Insertion of an unresolved gluon

The insertion of an unresolved gluon was already described in our earlier work
Chen:2022ktf
. From a practical standpoint, it is the simplest type of insertion, since it affects all the external partons in the same way. It occurs within antenna functions coming from the integration over a soft or collinear gluon, namely
A
-,
D
-, and
F
-type three-parton tree-level antenna functions.
In Table
18
we indicate the transition rules to convert integrated dipoles and corresponding integrated antenna functions to the unintegrated antenna functions. The inserted unresolved gluon is denoted with the label
u_{g}
.

f_{3,g}^{0}(1_{g},u_{g},i_{g})

\mathcal{F}_{3,gg}^{0}(s_{12})

Table 18
:
Replacement rules to convert integrated antenna functions to their unintegrated counterparts for the insertion of an unresolved gluon (denoted with
u_{g}
) between the pair of hard radiators.

In a practical implementation, one typically requires a numerical indexing of the partons: for an
(n+2)
-parton LO process, one can chose
u_{g}=n+3
. We clarify how the insertion is performed with an example. We consider the following term which is part of the leading-colour NLO virtual subtraction term for
pp\to 4j
production, specifically for
q{\bar{q}}\to gggg
:

\displaystyle-\left(J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},3_{g})+J_{2}^{(1)}(3_{g},4_{g})+J_{2}^{(1)}(4_{g},5_{g})+J_{2}^{(1)}(5_{g},6_{g})+J_{2}^{(1)}(2_{\bar{q}},6_{g})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}).

(117)

To insert the unresolved gluon
7_{g}
, we can rely on Table
18
to first convert the integrated dipoles to integrated antenna functions, and then replace them with their unintegrated counterparts. We obtain:

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle d^{0}_{3,q}(1_{q},7_{g},3_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(\widetilde{(17)}_{q},\widetilde{(37)}_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(\widetilde{p_{37}},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6})

(118)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle f^{0}_{3}(3_{g},7_{g},4_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},\widetilde{(37)}_{g},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(\widetilde{p_{37}},\widetilde{p_{47}},p_{5},p_{6})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle f^{0}_{3}(4_{g},7_{g},5_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},\widetilde{(57)}_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},\widetilde{p_{47}},\widetilde{p_{57}},p_{6})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle f^{0}_{3}(5_{g},7_{g},6_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},\widetilde{(57)}_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},\widetilde{p_{57}},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle d^{0}_{3,q}(2_{\bar{q}},7_{g},6_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},\widetilde{(27)}_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}}).

As another example, we consider a specific contribution to the subleading-colour part of the virtual subtraction terms:

\displaystyle-2\left(J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{{\bar{q}}})-J_{2}^{(1)}(1_{q},6_{g})-J_{2}^{(1)}(2_{\bar{q}},4_{g})+J_{2}^{(1)}(4_{g},6_{g})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}};1_{q},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}),

(119)

where we have an interference term between the two colour orderings
(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})
and
(1_{q},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})
, accompanied by a non-trivial dipole structure. Repeating the insertion steps yields:

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 2A^{0}_{3,q{\bar{q}}}(1_{q},7_{g},2_{\bar{q}})A^{2q,0}_{6}(\widetilde{(17)}_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},\widetilde{(27)}_{\bar{q}};\widetilde{(17)}_{q},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},3_{g},\widetilde{(27)}_{\bar{q}})

(120)

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt\times J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2d^{0}_{3,q}(1_{q},7_{g},6_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(\widetilde{(17)}_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},2_{\bar{q}};\widetilde{(17)}_{q},4_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt\times J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2d^{0}_{3,q}(2_{\bar{q}},7_{g},4_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},5_{g},6_{g},\widetilde{(27)}_{\bar{q}};1_{q},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},5_{g},6_{g},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt\times J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},\widetilde{p_{47}},p_{5},p_{6})

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 2f^{0}_{3}(4_{g},7_{g},6_{g})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},2_{\bar{q}};1_{q},\widetilde{(47)}_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},3_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt\times J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},\widetilde{p_{47}},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})\,.

The resulting expressions are part of the NLO real subtraction term for
q{\bar{q}}\to gggg
. Let’s now assume that, instead of the particular terms considered in (
4.3
) and (
4.3
) one performs the insertion of the unresolved gluon
7_{g}
within the full virtual subtraction term for
q{\bar{q}}\to gggg
, namely summing over all possible colour orderings. The result obtained this way will still not contain the entire unresolved behaviour of the real matrix element associated to gluon
7_{g}
becoming soft or collinear to hard partons. In particular, it will contain the entirety of the soft limits and the quark-gluon collinear limits, but for each possible gluon-gluon collinear pair
(i_{g},7_{g})
it will only have part of the collinear limit, due to the construction of the three-parton tree-level sub-antenna functions. The full collinear behaviour is restored when an analogous term with unresolved gluon
i_{g}
is added.

To be more precise, the procedure described before for the insertion of the unresolved gluon
7_{g}
generates a function of the external real-kinematics momenta:

f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6};p_{7})\,,

(121)

where we isolated the last argument, since it is the one corresponding to the newly inserted gluon, which, for the moment, is the only one with infrared divergences associated to it. The full singular behaviour is obtained, in this specific example with five final-state gluons, taking:

\displaystyle f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6};p_{7})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6},p_{7};p_{3})

\displaystyle f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{5},p_{6},p_{7},p_{3};p_{4})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{6},p_{7},p_{3},p_{4};p_{5})

\displaystyle f(p_{1},p_{2},p_{7},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5};p_{6})

.

(122)

The combination above allows each gluon to play the role of the unresolved parton, effectively reproducing all the soft and collinear limits in the considered process. We note that the actual ordering of the hard gluons in each instance of the function
f
is irrelevant, since it already contains a sum over all possible colour orderings. Indeed, in (
4.3
), the only relevant position for a gluon momentum is the last one.

Insertion of an unresolved quark-antiquark pair

The insertion of quark-antiquark pair is necessary when the LO process has gluons in the final state.
E
- and
G
-type antenna functions address such configuration. While the insertion of an unresolved gluon proceeds just by adding a parton on top of the LO partonic content, in this case we have a final-state gluon which is converted into a quark-antiquark pair.

We consider a
(n+2)
-parton process with
{n_{g}}
final-state gluons, labelled as
(i,\dots,i+{n_{g}}-1)
, while we temporarily label the new quark and antiquark with
u_{q}
and
u_{\bar{q}}
. Any final-state gluon is allowed to split into
(u_{q},u_{\bar{q}})
. To take this into account, we apply to the integrated expression the insertions described in Table
19
. In particular, within the FF
G_{3}^{0}
antenna function, either gluon
i_{g}
or
j_{g}
can split into
(u_{q},u_{\bar{q}})
and both contributions are taken into account. Since we are inserting a quark-antiquark pair which is allowed to become collinear, it is convenient to directly perform a symmetrization over the quark and the antiquark indices already at this level.

J_{2}^{(1)}

\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}

Table 19
:
Replacement rules to convert integrated antenna functions to their unintegrated counterparts when the final-state gluon splits into an unresolved quark-antiquark pair, denoted with
(u_{q},u_{\bar{q}})
. Symmetrization over the inserted quark-antiquark pair is always considered.

After the insertion, we need to relabel the partonic indices. First of all, the real kinematics has one gluon less, so the gluons are re-assigned to indices
(i,\dots,i+{n_{g}}-2)
. The actual way this relabelling happens is not relevant, since, once again, we will sum over all possible colour orderings. Then, the newly added quark
u_{q}
is assigned to the free index
i+{n_{g}}-1
, while the antiquark is indexed as
n+3
. This strategy ensures that a quark-antiquark pair is consistently inserted in any possible ordering of the original set of gluons. As a result, the subtraction term for each limit is generated exactly
{n_{g}}
times, and we must normalize by
1/{n_{g}}
.

If the LO process already has hard quark-antiquark pairs, for the insertion procedure we assume the newly added pair has a flavour different from any other pair already present in the process. Indeed, collinear
q\parallel{\bar{q}}
singularities are only present at the squared amplitude level when
q
and
{\bar{q}}
belong to the same fermionic line. Therefore, the
q\parallel{\bar{q}}
singular behaviour of real emissions corrections with two or more identical-flavour fermionic lines are described by the same structures required for the different-flavour case.

For an example, we can consider once again the
q{\bar{q}}\to gggg
process in the context of the NLO correction to
pp\to 4j
. In this case
{n_{g}}=4
and the final-state gluons are labelled with indices
3_{g}
,
4_{g}
,
5_{g}
and
6_{g}
. After the insertion, we will have three gluons labelled as
3_{g}
,
4_{g}
and
5_{g}
, a quark
6_{q}
and an antiquark
7_{\bar{q}}
. We take the following term from the leading-
N_{f}
contribution to the virtual subtraction term:

\displaystyle-\left(\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(1_{q},3_{g})+\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(3_{g},4_{g})+\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(4_{g},5_{g})+\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(5_{g},6_{g})+\hat{J_{2}}^{(1)}(2_{\bar{q}},6_{g})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}).

(123)

The insertion yields, after summing over all gluons and multiplying with an overall factor
1/{n_{g}}=1/4
:

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{16}E_{3,q}^{0}(1_{q},6_{q},7_{\bar{q}})A^{2q,0}_{6}(\widetilde{(16)}_{q},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},3_{g},4_{g},5_{g},2_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{16}E_{3,q}^{0}(2_{\bar{q}},6_{q},7_{\bar{q}})A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},4_{g},5_{g},\widetilde{(26)}_{\bar{q}})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{16}G_{3}^{0}(3_{g},6_{q},7_{\bar{q}})\Big[\Big.A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},\widetilde{(36)}_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},4_{g},5_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04872ptA^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},\widetilde{(36)}_{g},4_{g},5_{g},2_{\bar{q}})\Big.\Big]J_{4}^{(4)}(\widetilde{p_{36}},p_{4},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{16}G_{3}^{0}(4_{g},6_{q},7_{\bar{q}})\Big[\Big.A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(46)}_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},5_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04872ptA^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},\widetilde{(46)}_{g},5_{g},2_{\bar{q}})\Big.\Big]J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},\widetilde{p_{46}},p_{5},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{16}G_{3}^{0}(5_{g},6_{q},7_{\bar{q}})\Big[\Big.A^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},\widetilde{(56)}_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},2_{\bar{q}})

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04872ptA^{2q,0}_{6}(1_{q},3_{g},4_{g},\widetilde{(67)}_{g},\widetilde{(56)}_{g},2_{\bar{q}})\Big.\Big]J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4}\widetilde{p_{56}},\widetilde{p_{67}})

\displaystyle+\left(6\leftrightarrow 7\right).

(124)

The insertion proceeds analogously even beyond the leading-
N_{f}
contributions, with squared coherent partial amplitudes possibly replaced by incoherent interferences between different colour orderings. Contrary to the gluon insertion case, no additional sum over permutations of real-kinematics momenta is required here, because the obtained expression completely encapsulates the singular behaviour in the
q\parallel{\bar{q}}
limit.

Insertion of an unresolved parton in IC limits

IC limits at NLO occur when a final-state quark (antiquark), denoted in the following by
u_{q}
(
u_{\bar{q}}
) becomes collinear to either an initial-state gluon (
g\to q
) or initial-state quark (antiquark) of the same flavour (
q\to g
). The appropriate replacement rules for IC insertions are listed in Table
20
, where parton
1
is the one involved in the identity-changing limit, while the second parton in the integrated dipoles (antenna functions) is just a spectator. Symmetrization over the collinear quark-antiquark pair is considered for G- and
E
-type antenna functions.

J_{2}^{(1)}

\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}

:

Analogously to the insertion of a quark-antiquark pair, IC insertions require a suitable relabelling of the parton indices within the affected structures. The quark (antiquark)
u_{q}
(
u_{\bar{q}}
) responsible for the IC limit is labelled with the proper index that it has in the real-emission kinematics, while the indices of the other final-state partons are shifted accordingly. The procedure can be clarified with an example: we consider the LO process
qg\to qggg
and we address a
g\to q
IC limits. Namely we aim to construct the subtraction term for the real-emission process
gg\to q{\bar{q}}ggg
, for the limit when the final state antiquark becomes collinear to the initial state gluon. We have
u_{\bar{q}}=4
, which means that the additional final-state gluons, in the transition from the LO to the real kinematics, must have their indices shifted from
(4,5,6)
to
(5,6,7)
.

We start from the following contribution in the IC component of the virtual subtraction term:

-J_{2,g\to q}^{(1)}(1_{q},2_{g})M_{6}^{0,2q}(1_{q},2_{g},3_{q},4_{g},5_{g},6_{g})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{4},p_{5},p_{6}),

(125)

where
M_{6}^{0,2q}
represents the full LO matrix element. The transition to the unintegrated level, including the appropriate relabelling of momenta gives:

d_{3,gg}^{0}(4_{\bar{q}},1_{g},2_{g})M_{6}^{0,2q}(\widetilde{(14)}_{q},\widetilde{(24)}_{g},3_{q},5_{g},6_{g},7_{g})J_{4}^{(4)}(p_{3},p_{5},p_{6},p_{7}).

(126)

The same logic applies to contributions beyond the leading-colour approximation and to
g\to q
collinear limits. Also in this case, the generated expressions completely cover the considered IC collinear limit and no additional sum over permutations of the external momenta is needed.

The procedure described above, taking into account all different types of insertions, is comprehensively summarized by the following notation:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right].

(127)

The
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator systematically applies the transition from integrated to unintegrated quantities, as well as the required adjustments, such as momentum mapping, relabelling and appropriate sum over permutations of the external momenta.
We summarize the action of the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator in Figure
1
.

Removal of splitting kernels from
J_{2}^{(1)}

Insertion of a single unresolved parton:
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\to X_{3}^{0}

Relabelling/mapping of momenta

Fix overall factors

Figure 1
:
General outline of the generation of the real subtraction term from the virtual subtraction term, via the application of the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator.

We will now clarify some important aspects related to the procedure we have described. First of all, we observe that the real subtraction term obtained by (
127
) satisfies (
6
)
by construction
. Indeed, the analytical integration of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
is realized in the context of antenna subtraction by integrating the antenna functions appearing in it. Since the action of the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator is based on the univocal correspondence in (
115
), by converting the unintegrated antenna functions in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
into their integrated counterparts (analytical integration as in the traditional approach), one would obtain the exact same expression for the virtual subtraction term used as the input of (
127
), modulo the mass factorization counterterm which is added later. In this regard, equation (
127
) should be viewed as the unambiguous inverse operation with respect to (
6
). Namely, up to mass factorization counterterms, one has:

\int_{1}\mathcal{I}ns\left[f\right]=f,

(128)

where
f
is used instead of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
to emphasize the generality of such property.

The momentum mapping relating the
(n+3)
- and the
(n+2)
-momentum sets is induced in each term of an expression like (
105
) by the antenna function. The specific form of the mapping does not affect the final integrated result, provided it preserves the exact factorization of the antenna phase space
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
. Therefore, when the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator is applied, the unintegrated antenna functions straightforwardly dictate which kind of momentum mapping is required within the associated reduced matrix element, namely what are the hard radiators, the unresolved parton and the kinematical configuration (FF, IF or II). The specific form of the mapping can then be chosen at the level of the numerical implementation. As in the traditional antenna subtraction approach, we always consider the mappings described in
Kosower:2002su
.

What is in principle left to discuss is whether
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
obtained via the outlined approach correctly subtracts
all
the infrared-divergent behaviour of the real-emission correction. First of all, we remark once again that the relation in (
6
) is automatically fulfilled. Although equation (
6
) alone is not sufficient to claim that
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NLO}}}
works as intended, it is a necessary condition for the cancellation of the infrared singularities between real and virtual corrections and significantly restricts the form of the real subtraction term. In addition, with (
127
) we are effectively dressing each pair of hard radiators with an unresolved emission, taking then into account all possible unresolved limits of the real-emission matrix element. The potential double-counting of an unresolved configuration is prevented by the fact that NLO antenna functions have well defined unresolved partons and hard radiators. As discussed in the insertion examples above, the correct infrared behaviour in collinear limits is only recovered when a sum over all potentially unresolved partons is performed, due to the ambiguity in the identification of a hard emitter and an unresolved parton in such limits. We anticipate that at NNLO some of these features are less clearly manifest in the final form of the subtraction terms. This is the reason why we will need to suitably decompose the subtraction framework into several structures with specific roles in order to define a process-independent strategy to assemble the subtraction terms. The knowledge and insights accumulated during previous applications of the traditional antenna subtraction method will be particularly relevant for the formulation of the colourful antenna subtraction approach at NNLO.

We conclude by mentioning here that angular correlations in collinear limits require particular attention. Such terms average to zero after integration over the phase space of the collinear particles, yielding no explicit poles at the virtual level. Within the antenna subtraction method, angular terms are never subtracted locally. As explained in detail in
Glover:2010kwr
, a suitable point-by-point angular average is performed to locally enforce the vanishing of the angular terms. For this reason, the absence of explicit poles associated to such divergences at the virtual level does not represent an issue for the colourful antenna subtraction method.

4.4
Observations

We carefully treated the insertion of unresolved partons at NLO because the techniques established at NLO are repeatedly used at NNLO as well. Indeed, the majority of the NNLO subtraction terms can be generated through the iterated application of
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
to integrated quantities. This was recently observed also in the context of the nested soft-collinear subtraction scheme
Devoto:2023rpv
. The only exception is represented by configurations that require a simultaneous double insertion of unresolved partons.

The construction of the NLO real subtraction term concludes the description of the colourful antenna method at this perturbative order.
As in the traditional subtraction schemes, the key requirement is the analytical integrability of the real subtraction term over the phase space of the unresolved radiation. In addition, here it is crucial that the virtual subtraction term is expressed in a language suitable for the
unintegration
procedure. The correspondence between integrated antenna functions, collected in (
70
), and their unintegrated versions guarantees this property.

We remark that the knowledge of the unintegrated antenna functions required for the construction of the real subtraction term is a crucial premise for the application of this method. Indeed, the described unintegration procedure allows for a systematic assembly of such ingredients and not for an actual direct generation of the structures required to remove the infrared divergences of real emission corrections, such as eikonal factors or splitting kernels. This observation, of course, applies to the NNLO case too.

5
Colourful antenna subtraction at NNLO

In the following, we document the colourful antenna subtraction method at NNLO. We recall the structure of the NNLO correction to a partonic sub-process initiated by partons
a
and
b
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{n}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]

(129)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{n+1}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RV}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{n+2}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{RR}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

with

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\int_{2}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\,.

(130)

As for the NLO case, typically one would study the infrared-divergent behaviour of the real-virtual and double-real matrix elements to construct
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{VS}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
first, which are then integrated and combined with the mass factorization counterterms to complete the subtraction infrastructure. In contrast, the application of the colourful antenna subtraction method at NNLO begins by addressing the double-virtual correction in colour space. Once the double-virtual subtraction term is constructed, the real-virtual and double-real subtraction terms are generated via the insertion of unresolved partons, exploiting the relations between integrated and unintegrated structures in the subtraction terms in (
2.1.2
).

To outline the colourful antenna subtraction method at NNLO, we summarize its procedure in Figure
2
. Single descendant red arrows represent the transition from an integrated quantity to its unintegrated counterpart by means of the insertion of an unresolved parton. Two disjoint red arrows indicate the iterated insertion of two unresolved partons, while two connected red arrows indicate the simultaneous insertion of two unresolved partons.

The definitions of each component are listed in Table
21
.

5.1
NNLO mass factorization in colour space

We start by casting the NNLO mass factorization counterterm in a form which suits the colourful approach. At NNLO, as indicated in (
19
) and (
2.1.2
), we have two different contributions: the double-virtual and the real-virtual mass factorization terms.

5.1.1
Double virtual mass factorization term

The double-virtual mass factorization term at NNLO reads:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\sum_{c,d}\Bigg\{\Bigg.\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)\right]

(131)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\Big[\Big.\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;cd}\left({x_{1},x_{2}}\right)-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\dfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;\alpha\beta}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{\alpha\beta;cd}\right](x_{1},x_{2})\Big.\Big]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\},

and collects contributions factorizing onto an
n
-particle phase space, where
n
is the final-state multiplicity of the LO process. As usual we separate the IP and IC components:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

(132)

As in Section
4.1
at NLO, we express the two-loop identity-preserving mass factorization kernels in colour space as:

\overline{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)=\overline{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{aa,\text{full}}\left(x_{1}\right)\delta(1-x_{2})+\overline{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{bb,\text{full}}\left(x_{2}\right)\delta(1-x_{1}),

(133)

where

\overline{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{aa,\text{full}}\left(x_{i}\right)=-\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{aa,\text{full}}\left({x_{i}}\right)\dfrac{1}{C_{a}}\sum_{j\neq i}\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j},\quad i=1,2.

(134)

Hence, we can write the IP component of the mass factorization counterterm as:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(135)

\displaystyle\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt-2\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)\right](x_{1},x_{2})|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt+\dfrac{1}{2}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left[\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\otimes\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\right](x_{1},x_{2})|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\overline{\mathbf{\Gamma}}^{(2)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\},

where we inserted the expression of the identity-preserving virtual subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,\mathrm{IP}}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}
given in (
113
).

The IC part of the two-loop mass factorization counterterms is not expressed in colour space. It reads:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,2,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\Bigg\{\Bigg.\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)\right]

(136)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\Bigg[\Bigg.\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab),(cd)}\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;ef}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{ef;cd}\right](x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;cd}(x_{1},x_{2})-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\right](x_{1},x_{2})+\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{cd;cd}\right](x_{1},x_{2})\Bigg.\Bigg]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab)}\Bigg[\Bigg.\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;ef}\otimes\Gamma^{(1)}_{ef;ab}\right](x_{1},x_{2})+\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;ef;ab}(x_{1},x_{2})\Bigg.\Bigg]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{LO}}},

where the last line includes the flip-flopping contributions, with the flip-flopping kernel defined as

\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{ab;ef;ab}\left({x_{1},x_{2}}\right)=\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{a\to e\to a}\left({1}\right)\delta(1-x_{2})\delta_{ae}+\overline{\Gamma}^{(2)}_{b\to f\to b}\left({2}\right)\delta(1-x_{1})\delta_{bf}.

(137)

5.1.2
Real virtual mass factorization term

The real-virtual mass factorization term is given by
21
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{c,d}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right).

and can be split into two contributions:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(138)

with

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{c,d}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}},

(139)

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{c,d}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{ab;cd}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}.

(140)

Both
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
can be split into their IP and IC components. In analogy with (
110
), the IP component of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
can be rewritten in colour space as:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{N}^{RV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\int\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})\,J_{n}^{(n+1)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n+1})

(141)

\displaystyle\times\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+3}^{0}|\mathbf{\Gamma}^{(1)}_{ab;ab}\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+3}^{0}},

where
\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n+3}^{0}}
indicates the single-real correction amplitude and
\mathcal{N}^{RV}_{NNLO}
is the appropriate overall coefficient at the real-virtual level:

\mathcal{N}^{RV}_{NNLO}=s_{RV}(4\pi\alpha_{s})\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\mathcal{N}_{LO},\quad\text{with}\quad s_{RV}=s_{R}.

(142)

Terms in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
have a structure like
\Gamma^{(1)}_{ij}X_{3}^{0}a_{n+2}^{0}
. As we show in Section
5.3.2
below, this term is used to reconstruct one-loop integrated dipoles that are needed in the real-virtual subtraction term to remove the explicit poles of one-loop reduced matrix elements.

5.2
NNLO double-virtual subtraction term

The double-virtual subtraction term at NNLO
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, reproduces the explicit poles of the two-loop matrix element and contains the double-virtual mass factorization counterterm. In the following we see how to construct
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in a general way relying on the results of the previous sections.

We focus first on the identity-preserving component. Using the dipole operators defined in Sections
3.3.1
and
3.3.2
, we can construct:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(143)

\displaystyle\times 2\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt-\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(2)}(\epsilon)|}-\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}(\epsilon)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\}.

One can verify that the expression above reproduces the same infrared singularity structure described by (
68
), thanks to the identities relating the
\epsilon
-poles of colour-stripped integrated dipoles and infrared insertion operators, given in equations (
3.3.2
), (
3.3.2
) and (
3.3.2
). We remark the presence of
\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}
in the last line, to compensate for the unphysical poles present in quark-gluon integrated dipoles. Equation (
143
) provides a fully general result for the removal of explicit double-virtual singularities in terms of integrated antenna functions.

According to the usual decomposition of the double-virtual subtraction term
Currie:2013vh
, we split (
143
) into the following contributions:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(144)

\displaystyle\times 2\,\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\Big.\Big\},

which collects dipole insertions within the one-loop correction,

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(145)

\displaystyle\times 2\,\Big\{\Big.-\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\Big.\Big\},

which addresses double dipole insertions at tree-level and

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(146)

\displaystyle\times 2\,\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(2)}(\epsilon)|}-{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}(\epsilon)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\},

which contains two-loop integrated dipoles. We further decompose
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
into

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{0},\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(147)

\displaystyle\times 2\,\Big\{\Big.-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\Big.\Big\},

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1},\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{n}(p_{3},\dots,p_{n+2};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})

(148)

\displaystyle\times 2\,\Big\{\Big.\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\Big.\Big\},

where we separated the contribution of the one-loop amplitude from the
\beta_{0}
term, which only contains tree-level amplitudes.

We also introduce a decomposition of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in (
145
) according to the colour connections among the hard radiators in the two integrated dipoles. After the evaluation of the colour algebra,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
has the form:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij,kl}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(k,l)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};.,i,.,j,.,k,.,l,.),

(149)

which we split into the following contributions
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
:

•

colour-connected (
c.c.
) contributions, where the pair of hard radiators coincides in the two integrated dipoles:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP},c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};.,i,.,j,.),

(150)

•

almost colour-connected (
a.c.c.
) contributions, where the two integrated dipoles share one hard radiator
i
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP},a.c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ijk}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,k)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};.,i,.,j,.,k,.),

(151)

•

colour-unconnected (
c.u.
) contributions, where there is no overlap between the hard radiators in the two integrated dipoles:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,\mathrm{IP},u.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij}\sum_{kl\neq ij}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(k,l)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};.,i,.,j,.,k,.,l,.).

(152)

Finally,
we label the contributions in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
according to the different types of integrated antenna functions which appear in them:

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
: integrated four-parton tree-level antenna functions;

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
: integrated three-parton one-loop antenna functions;

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,{\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
: convolution of two integrated three-parton tree-level antenna functions;

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\beta_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
:
\beta_{0}/\epsilon
terms, with a single integrated three-parton tree-level antenna function.

Other structures appearing in the two-loop integrated dipoles are not listed here since they originate from mass factorization and do not propagate beyond the double-virtual level. Such structures are two-loop mass factorization kernels, convolutions of two one-loop mass factorization kernels and convolutions of a one-loop mass factorization kernel with an integrated three-parton tree-level antenna function.

The identity-changing part of the double-virtual subtraction term reflects the structure of the IC mass factorization counterterm:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\Bigg\{\Bigg.\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\left[\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)\right]

(153)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\Bigg[\Bigg.\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab),(cd)}\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{ef;cd}^{(1)}\right](x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82234pt+\,\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})-\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\Bigg.\Bigg]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab)}\Bigg[\Bigg.\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{ef;ab}^{(1)}\right](x_{1},x_{2})+\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef;ab}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})\Bigg.\Bigg]\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{LO}}}.

The expression above collects all the structures in the double-virtual IC mass factorization counterterm and combines them with IC integrated NNLO antenna functions. It is overall free from infrared singularities. We notice that in the first line, the difference between the virtual correction and its corresponding subtraction term has no
\epsilon
-poles. Therefore, even if we were to express the virtual subtraction term in colour space to expose its singularity structure, it would
not be necessary to do so in this context.

Despite not being expressed in colour space, the IC double-virtual subtraction term exhibits analogous structures to the ones present in its IP counterpart. We can indeed decompose it in the following components:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IC},a_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\left(\dfrac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\right)\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}},

(154)

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IC},a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=
