\displaystyle-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})

(155)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\left(\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{V}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}\right)\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}},

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IC},b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Bigg\{\Bigg.

(156)

\displaystyle\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab),(cd)}\left[\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{ef;cd}^{(1)}\right](x_{1},x_{2})\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab)}\left[\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef}^{(1)}\otimes\mathcal{J}_{ef;ab}^{(1)}\right](x_{1},x_{2})\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\},

with a subsequent decomposition into colour-connected, almost colour-connected and colour-unconnected contributions as for the IP counterparts, and

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,\mathrm{IC},c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\Bigg\{\Bigg.\sum_{(cd)\neq(ab)}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{cd,\mathrm{LO}}}

(157)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\sum_{(ef)\neq(ab)}\mathcal{J}_{ab;ef;ab}^{(2)}(x_{1},x_{2})\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}_{ab,\mathrm{LO}}}\Bigg.\Bigg\},

which can be further decomposed according to the specific type of integrated structures appearing in each term.

Since the IP and IC components of the double-virtual subtraction term exhibit the same structures in terms of integrated dipoles, in the derivation of the real-virtual and double-real subtraction terms we can always consider the full combination of IP and IC terms, given that the required manipulations to translate integrated quantities to unintegrated ones are analogous. For this reason, in the following sections we will often drop the superscripts IP and IC, indicating that we are working with the complete subtraction terms. We will restore the labels only when needed.

At NLO, once the virtual subtraction term is obtained, it is straightforward to systematically construct the real subtraction term. At NNLO, the structure of the subtraction is significantly more involved, due to the presence of two additional layers besides the double-virtual correction: real-virtual and double-real.

5.3
NNLO real-virtual subtraction term

The real-virtual subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
cancels the explicit
\epsilon
-poles in the real-virtual matrix element, contains the real-virtual mass factorization counterterm and removes the divergent behaviour in single-unresolved infrared limits. In the following, we illustrate how the real-virtual subtraction term can be generated in the context of the colourful antenna subtraction method.

We anticipate that the current section is the densest one, with the construction of the real-virtual subtraction term really being the core step of the colourful antenna subtraction method. This may sound surprising, since we are dealing with just a single unresolved emission. However, as we show in Section
5.4
, the price to pay for a particularly straightforward generation of the double-real subtraction term for a generic process, is that we must carefully arrange all the structures in the real-virtual subtraction term in order to achieve this. The reader may notice that some components of the real-virtual subtraction term seem redundant, and that significant cancellations are possible when one considers the full expressions. This is indeed true, but, once again, to properly prepare the derivation of the double-real subtraction term, we are forced to isolate individual structures, even if they yield a simpler result when summed. Of course, such separation is necessary only for the illustration purposes. In any practical implementation of the subtraction terms, one can consider the sum over all components, likely resulting in more compact expressions.

The construction of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is performed in two main steps:

•

integrated terms are translated from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
inserting an unresolved parton, via the application of
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
, in complete analogy to what is done at NLO;

•

additional terms are systematically generated to remove leftover explicit and implicit infrared singularities.

Any additional contribution which is added at the real-virtual level and does not have a direct correspondence to terms in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
will eventually generate corresponding terms at the double-real level after the insertion of a second unresolved parton. We note that not the entirety of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
will be translated to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, some terms will undergo a double insertion and directly move from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

We recall the usual decomposition of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in the context of antenna subtraction
Currie:2013vh
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

(158)

The meaning of this decomposition is the following:

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
removes the explicit poles of the real-virtual matrix element;

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
reproduces the divergent behaviour of the real-virtual matrix element in single unresolved limits, but requires additional contributions to ensure
\epsilon
-finiteness;

•

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
removes the overlap in the
single unresolved behaviour between the two terms above.

We summarize in Figure
3
the interplay between different contributions in the real-virtual subtraction term and the matrix element, denoted with ME. Green boxes are free from explicit
\epsilon
-poles, namely the quantities they contain are constructed to be
\epsilon
-finite. Light-blue boxes denote combinations of terms which are not divergent in single-unresolved limits. One can observe that the sum of the matrix element and the subtraction terms is overall free from any infrared singularity. Each term appearing in Figure
3
is discussed in detail in the following.

Figure 3
:
Structure of the infrared-subtracted real-virtual correction. Green boxes enclose
\epsilon
-finite quantities, while inside light-blue ones the infrared-divergent behaviour has been completely subtracted. The combination of the matrix element (ME) and the subtraction terms is free from any explicit and implicit infrared singularity.

5.3.1
d
\sigma^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

This component of the real-virtual subtraction term removes the explicit poles of the
(n+3)
-particle one-loop matrix element. Moreover, it contains the mass factorization contribution
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
given in (
139
). It is indicated as the orange box in Figure
3
. The construction of
\sigma^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
is completely analogous to the one adopted for the NLO virtual subtraction term in Section
4.2
, with the only difference given by the presence of an additional particle. Hence, we have:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a,\mathrm{IP}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(159)

with

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a,\mathrm{IP}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{RV}_{NNLO}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\text{d}\Phi_{(}p_{3},\dots,p_{n+3};x_{1}p_{1},x_{2}p_{2})J_{n}^{(n+1)}(\left\{p\right\}_{n+1})

(160)

\displaystyle\times 2\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+3}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|},

and

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a,\mathrm{IC}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{s}\overline{C}(\epsilon)}{2\pi}\right)\sum_{(c,d)\neq(a,b)}\int{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{1}}{x_{1}}}{\dfrac{\text{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}}}\mathcal{J}_{ab;cd}^{(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\,\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{R}_{cd,\mathrm{NLO}}}.

(161)

This term is not directly related to any contribution at the double-virtual level. Indeed, its unintegrated counterpart removes the single-unresolved behaviour of the double-real matrix element and will be subtracted there.

5.3.2
d
\sigma^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

This term reproduces the divergent behaviour of the real-virtual matrix element in single unresolved infrared limits. The subtraction of infrared divergences from a one loop matrix element is more involved than the one required at tree-level. In particular, along with tree-level antenna functions and reduced one-loop matrix elements (tree
\times
loop), suitable combinations of three-particle one-loop antenna functions and tree-level reduced matrix elements (loop
\times
tree) have to be used. Both these structures are present, in their integrated form, in the double-virtual subtraction term, since they describe the emission of soft and collinear particles from the one-loop amplitude. It is indeed possible to systematically generate these terms at the real-virtual level starting from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, as we show in the following. According to
Currie:2013vh
, we introduce a suitable decomposition of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(162)

where the elements on the right-hand-side respectively contain (tree
\times
loop) contributions, (loop
\times
tree) contributions and suitable terms needed to ensure the correct renormalization of loop quantities in the real-virtual subtraction term. Moreover,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
need to be made
\epsilon
-finite including additional terms, as shown in Figure
3
.
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
are indicated in Figure
3
by the two central green boxes.

d
\sigma^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

We first focus on
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which addresses configurations where the infrared divergences can be captured by the same tree-level unresolved factors used at NLO. The respective integrated counterpart appears in the double-virtual subtraction term as a combination of a one-loop integrated dipole (integrated tree-level three-parton antenna function) and a one-loop reduced matrix element in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. The procedure of inserting an unresolved parton at this level is the same as the one depicted in Section
4.3
, with the tree-level amplitudes replaced by the one-loop ones. The result has the (tree
\times
loop) structure:

\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\sim\sum_{ij,u}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\,X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\,a_{n+2}^{1}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.\}),

(163)

where
u
denotes the inserted unresolved parton. Even if the used notation is technically only appropriate in the context of unresolved-gluon insertions, we rely on it here and in the following examples for the sake of simplicity.

Terms in
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]
partially take care of the divergent behaviour of the real-virtual matrix element. However, the one-loop colour interferences
a_{n+2}^{1}
contain explicit
\epsilon
-poles which must be removed to ensure the finiteness of the real-virtual subtraction term. This only affects the IP sector of the subtraction terms, with the IC one being free of any explicit
\epsilon
-poles.

Omitting the phase space integration and other factors which are not relevant for the current discussion, these poles read:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\right\}

(164)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\Bigg[\Bigg.2\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}|}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\Bigg[\Bigg.2\sum_{ij}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}+\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{4\,\mathcal{I}ns\Bigg[\Bigg.\sum_{ij}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\text{Re}\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{1}}\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\,\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\text{Re}\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{1}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\,\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\text{Re}\left(\braket{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\left(\sum_{kl}\left(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\right)\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{kl}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right)|\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\sum_{kl}\,\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\text{Re}\left[\mathcal{I}^{(1)}_{kl}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\right]\right\}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\left(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}},

where
(\widetilde{n+2})
is a shorthand notation to indicate that the momenta of the external partons in the colour-ordered amplitudes underwent a suitable relabelling according to the action of the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator. In particular, the momenta associated to partons
k
and
l
, which enter in the one-loop infrared operator, belong to the relabelled set of momenta.

These poles can be reproduced systematically by
applying the unresolved parton insertion to the

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, namely within the double dipole structure:

\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\sim\mathcal{I}ns\left[-2\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\right].

(165)

We need to clarify how the unresolved parton insertion works in the presence of more than one integrated dipole. The
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator discards the splitting kernels from both the integrated dipoles
\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}
. The transition to unintegrated antenna functions is then performed in only one of the integrated dipoles (integrated antenna functions). The choice of integrated dipole should be done in such a way that any pair of hard radiators is addressed once and only once. In practice, this can be easily achieved by symmetrizing:

\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{a}\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{b}=\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{a}\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{b}+\dfrac{1}{2}\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{b}\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}_{a},

(166)

where the subscripts
a
and
b
have been introduced to distinguish the two dipoles, and then fixing the first dipole in each term of the symmetrized expression to be the one subjected to the insertion of an unresolved parton. Finally, the momenta relabelling has to be performed, not only within the colour interferences and the jet function, but also within the surviving integrated antenna functions. This applies in general at NNLO, namely the transition to higher multiplicities via momenta relabelling occurs within any function of the external momenta accompanying the integrated dipole (antenna function) which is converted into an unintegrated antenna function. With this, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[-2\,\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\right\}\sim 4\,\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)\otimes\mathbfcal{J}^{(1)}(\epsilon)|}\right]\right\}

(167)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\,\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\sum_{ij}\sum_{kl}\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(k,l)\braket{\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\left(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{n+2}^{0}}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\sum_{k,l}\mathcal{P}oles\left\{s_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}(s_{kl})\right\}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\left(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}},

where
s_{\mathcal{X}}
indicates the specific symmetry factor and sign relating integrated dipoles and antenna functions. The obtained results is already quite similar to (
164
), but one final manipulation is needed to compare the two expressions. The goal is to convert the integrated antenna function in (
167
) back to an integrated one-loop dipole. One can indeed verify that the mass factorization kernels which restore the integrated dipoles are precisely provided by
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. Hence, one has:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[-2\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right\}

(168)

\displaystyle\hskip-184.9429pt\sim 4\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\sum_{k,l}\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(k,l)\right\}\braket{\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}|\left(\bm{T}_{i}\cdot\bm{T}_{j}\right)\left(\bm{T}_{k}\cdot\bm{T}_{l}\right)|\mathcal{A}_{\widetilde{n+2}}^{0}},

where we note that the momenta
k
and
l
belong to the relabelled set according to the action of the insertion operator. We can finally use (
73
) to conclude that (
164
) and (
168
) are equivalent, so:

\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\right\}=\mathcal{P}oles\left\{-2\,\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right\}.

(169)

This relation provides a natural and general way to produce terms to remove the residual explicit poles in
\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\right\}
. Schematically, these terms look like:

-2\,\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}}\sim\sum_{ij,u}\sum_{kl}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(k,l)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.\}).

(170)

It is particularly convenient that this can be achieved by the action of the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator on pre-existent structures at the double-virtual level and fully absorbing
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

The final result for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
then reads:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-2\,\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(171)

which, thanks to (
169
), is free of explicit
\epsilon
-singularities. To compensate
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in the double-virtual subtraction term, we should add back a factor
+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]
, which will appear below in the generation of the real-virtual and double-real subtraction terms. In Figure
3
, the (tree
\times
loop) component of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is indicated by the label
X_{3}^{0}M^{1}
, while additional contributions to remove the
\epsilon
-poles are indicated by
X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{1}
.

As a cross-check for the strategy described above, we can extract the explicit singularity structure of the one-loop colour-ordered partial amplitudes, following closely the procedures used
for the real-virtual corrections to
pp\to jj
in
Chen:2022clm
.
For a general process, one can extract the amplitude-level pole structure via suitable projectors in colour space. A generic renormalized
n
-parton one-loop amplitude in colour space is given by:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{1}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=\sum_{c\in I^{1}}\mathbfcal{C}_{n,c}^{1}\,A^{1}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n}),

(172)

with the coefficients
A^{1}_{n,c}
being one-loop colour-ordered amplitudes. Each vector
\mathbfcal{C}_{n,c}^{1}
defines a direction in colour space. By linearly combining the generating vectors
\left\{\mathbfcal{C}_{n,c}^{\ell}\right\}_{c}
one can construct a projector
\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}
to single out the component of the full amplitude along a specific direction:

\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{1}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=A^{1}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n}).

(173)

One can then exploit the relation above to isolate the infrared poles of the colour-ordered amplitude
A^{1}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(A^{1}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\left(\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{1}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}\right)

(174)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\,\,\mathcal{P}oles\left(\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{1}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\,\,\bm{I}^{(1)}\left(\epsilon,\mu_{r}^{2}\right)\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})},

where we used that the projector commutes with the extraction of the poles. The obtained pole structure can then be inserted in (
163
) to
obtain
\mathcal{P}oles\left\{\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\right\}
, in order to validate (
169
).

d
\sigma^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

We consider now
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, namely (loop
\times
tree) contributions, addressing configurations which require novel one-loop unresolved factors to described the divergent behaviour due to the unresolved emission. The core part of this term is given by unintegrated three-parton one-loop antenna functions
X_{3}^{1}
combined with tree-level reduced matrix elements. The integrated counterpart of these terms is contained in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. Once again, we can insert an unresolved parton and obtain from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
the contribution needed at the real-virtual level. The replacement rules for integrated three-parton one-loop antenna functions are completely analogous to the ones followed by tree-level antenna functions and are listed in Appendix
A.1
. We label the resulting contribution
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},X_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. It has the following form:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},X_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\sim\sum_{ij,u}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.\}).

(175)

As it happened for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, the expression above contains explicit
\epsilon
-poles coming from the one-loop antenna functions. These singularities need to be removed to obtain a finite subtraction term. This can be done systematically since the singularity structure of the unintegrated three-parton one-loop antenna functions can be expressed by means of one-loop integrated dipoles and three-parton tree-level antenna functions
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
. The poles of a generic one-loop three-parton antenna function can be absorbed by the following replacement:

X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)\to X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)+\sum_{(l,m)=1}^{N_{X}}J_{2}^{(1)}(l,m)X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)-M_{X}J_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j),

(176)

where the sum in the second term runs over the pairs of colour-connected partons in the antenna configuration. The replacement is valid in any kinematics (FF, IF or II). The number of colour-connected pairs
N_{X}
, as well as the coefficient
M_{X}
depend on the specific antenna function. Values for
N_{X}
and
M_{X}
and the explicit replacements required for all the one-loop antenna functions are listed in Appendix
A.1
. The expression obtained inserting (
176
) in (
175
) is free of poles and becomes part of the real-virtual subtraction term.

To use a similar notation to
Currie:2013vh
, we label
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
the two additional blocks coming from the newly added terms in (
176
). As we will explain in detail in the following,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
can be related to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
after the insertion of an unresolved parton, while
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is a genuinely new contribution added at the real-virtual level, which needs to be compensated by its unintegrated counterpart at the double-real level. This can be noticed by looking at the arguments of the integrated dipoles appearing in the two blocks.
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
depends on mapped momenta, which come from the insertion of an extra unresolved parton in the
n
-particle phase space, where the double-virtual subtraction term lives. On the other hand, the integrated dipoles in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
depend on
(n+3)
-particle phase space momenta, which are not accessible at the double-virtual level. It is trivial to verify that the mass factorization kernels between the integrated dipoles in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
exactly cancel for any configuration of partons
i
,
j
and
u
. For bookkeeping purposes we label this mass factorization
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

The term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
can be constructed as follows:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-m_{X}\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(177)

where
m_{X}
is an integer. For purely gluonic processes, it was found previously
Chen:2022ktf
that
m_{X}=2
. Any multiple of
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]
that is not compensated by the double-virtual subtraction term will be re-introduced in the construction of
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
below.

In conclusion, the (loop
\times
tree) block is given by:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(178)

which is free of
\epsilon
-poles. In Figure
3
, the (loop
\times
tree) component of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is indicated by the label
X_{3}^{1}M^{0}
, while contributions in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
are generically indicated by
X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{0}
.

d
\sigma^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

The last contribution to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is required to fix the renormalization of one-loop antenna functions in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. To fix the correct renormalization of the one-loop antenna functions it is sufficient to perform the following replacements
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
:

\displaystyle X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)+\dfrac{b_{0}}{\epsilon}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\left(\left(\dfrac{\left|s_{iuj}\right|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}-1\right),

(179)

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle X_{3}^{1}(i,u,j)+\dfrac{b_{0,F}}{\epsilon}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\left(\left(\dfrac{\left|s_{iuj}\right|}{\mu_{r}^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}-1\right),

(180)

for the leading-colour and the fermionic-loop one-loop antenna functions. The subleading-colour one-loop antenna functions do not require any renormalization.
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is entirely constructed with terms coming from the double-virtual subtraction term:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\beta_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(181)

as can be easily checked keeping track of the coefficient
\beta_{0}
.

The contribution
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is not depicted explicitly in Figure
3
, since we assume it is absorbed in the (loop
\times
tree) term of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, when the three-parton one-loop antenna functions are evaluated at the correct renormalization scale.

5.3.3
d
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

The last block of the real-virtual subtraction term is
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. This contribution is significantly less straightforward to derive than
\sigma^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
and
\sigma^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
, discussed in the previous sections. The reason for this lies in less stringent constraints for the generation of new structures, which propagate to the double-real subtraction term. Typically, the correctness of the
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
term can only be fully assessed by cross-checking its influence at the real-virtual and double-real level. Moreover, the
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
contribution in general has an exiguous numerical impact, which makes it hard to clearly detect mistakes during the validation process. We also notice that the remainder of this section differs significantly from Section
4.4.3
of
Chen:2022ktf
, because the extension beyond gluonic processes required a reinterpretation of the derivation strategy. Indeed, the completely symmetric structure of the subtraction terms in the gluons-only case allowed for a straightforward generation of the
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
term, which is not directly applicable to other partonic configurations.

We begin by observing Figure
3
:
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
has to remove residual divergent behaviour present in the combination of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and the
X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{0}
terms of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and has to be individually free from
\epsilon
-poles. We address these two requirements in this order.

Removal of single-unresolved behaviour

The first requirement is fulfilled designing a preliminary candidate for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, denoted with
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, given by:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\Bigg|_{\text{singular}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\Bigg|_{X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{0}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\Bigg|_{X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{0}}\right],

(182)

where the first term represents the singular behaviour of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in single-unresolved limits, while the remaining contributions are simply identical to the terms with a
X_{3}^{0}J_{2}^{(1)}M^{0}
structure in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. By construction,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
trivially fulfils its purpose, but a systematic procedure to construct
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\big|_{\text{singular}}
must be defined. The caveat is to have
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\big|_{\text{singular}}
extracted by means of three-parton tree-level antenna functions, to produce a
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
which coherently fits within the subtraction terms.

The structure of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
discussed in Section
5.3.1
is the one for an NLO virtual subtraction term for a process with
(n+3)
partons:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij}J_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)a_{n+3}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,i,.,j,.\}).

(183)

The colour correlations
a_{n+3}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.\})
exhibit a divergent behaviour in single-unresolved limits. The expression in (
183
) is clearly not a physical matrix element, therefore we cannot reproduce its divergent behaviour with the same techniques we employed for the construction of NLO subtraction terms. Nevertheless, the full
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\big|_{\text{singular}}
can be obtained if in each term of (
183
) we replace the colour interference
a_{n+3}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.,i,.,j,.\})
with the description of its divergent behaviour in terms of antenna functions. In other words, we need a systematic procedure to construct a real subtraction term for an individual colour interference
a_{n+3}^{0}(c,c^{\prime};\{.\})
. Unfortunately, the paradigm of the colourful antenna subtraction: infer real emission subtraction terms from virtual structures, cannot be applied in this case. The reason lies in the fact that it is not possible to identify a one-to-one correspondence between single terms in the colour decomposition of the matrix elements for virtual and real corrections. Hence, for this specific task, we need to rely on the factorization properties of tree-level amplitudes in single-unresolved limits.

The strategy we adopt is similar to the one described in Section
5.3.2
for the extraction of the explicit singularities of one-loop colour-ordered amplitudes. We first exploit universal factorization properties of QCD to describe the singular behaviour of tree-level amplitudes and then rely on suitable projection operators to single out a specific direction in colour space, effectively extracting the singular behaviour of single colour-ordered amplitudes. We recall the decomposition of a
n
-parton tree-level amplitude:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=\sum_{c\in I^{0}}\mathbfcal{C}_{n,c}^{0}\,A^{0}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n}),

(184)

where the vectors
\left\{\mathbfcal{C}_{n,c}^{0}\right\}_{n}
span the entire
n
-parton tree-level colour space. We can construct projectors
\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}
which satisfy:

\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}=A^{0}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n}).

(185)

We now consider the emission of a soft gluon, labelled as usual with
u
. The factorization reads:

\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})}\xrightarrow{u\to 0}\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\bm{T}_{i}\dfrac{p_{i}^{\mu}}{p_{u}\cdot p_{i}}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n-1})},

(186)

where
\mu
is the Lorentz index associated to the soft gluon and
\bm{T}_{i}
are the colour charges of the hard partons. The
(n-1)
-parton amplitude on the RHS is obtained removing the soft gluon
u
from the partonic content of the original amplitude. If we are interested in the soft behaviour of a specific colour-ordered amplitude
A^{0}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})
, we can make use of the projectors defined above:

\displaystyle A^{0}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\xrightarrow{u\to 0}\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\bm{T}_{i}\dfrac{p_{i}^{\mu}}{p_{u}\cdot p_{i}}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n-1})}\right).

(187)

We can apply this result to a generic interference between amplitudes with colour-ordering
c
and
c^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pta^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n})=A^{0}_{n,c}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})^{\dagger}A^{0}_{n,c^{\prime}}(\left\{p\right\}_{n})\xrightarrow{u\to 0}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\left[\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\bm{T}_{i}\dfrac{p_{i}^{\mu}}{p_{u}\cdot p_{i}}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n-1})}\right)\right]^{\dagger}\left[\bm{\mathcal{P}}_{c^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\bm{T}_{j}\dfrac{p_{j}^{\mu}}{p_{u}\cdot p_{j}}\ket{\mathcal{A}_{n-1}^{0}(\left\{p\right\}_{n-1})}\right)\right].

(188)

The expression above can be evaluated contracting colour and Lorentz indices to obtain a real result describing the soft behaviour of a generic colour interference. The results, up to numerical factors, has the following form:

a^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n})\xrightarrow{u\to 0}\sum_{\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}}\sum_{ij}S^{0}_{iuj}a^{0}_{n-1}(\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime};.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.),

(189)

where the first sum runs over colour structures
\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
in the
(n-1)
-parton tree-level colour space and
S^{0}_{iuj}
is the soft eikonal factor given:

S^{0}_{ijk}=\dfrac{2s_{ik}}{s_{ij}s_{jk}}.

(190)

We can build on the result in (
189
) exchanging the eikonal factors with suitable three-parton tree level antenna functions. Indeed,
X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)
antenna functions, exactly reproduce the eikonal factor
S^{0}_{iuj}
when a soft gluon
u
is emitted between the pair of hard radiators
(i,j)
. The specific choice of antenna function depends on the species and the kinematical configurations of partons
i
and
j
. Additionally, antenna functions also contain the collinear behaviour in the limits
i\parallel u
and
j\parallel u
, with the collinear limit
g\parallel g
is not captured by a single
X_{3}^{0}
antenna function, but is distributed between pairs of antenna functions where the role of the hard and unresolved gluons are interchanged. We can therefore take the result in (
189
), with eikonal factors suitably replaced by antenna functions, and perform a sum over all possible choices of soft gluon
u
. The final result has the following structure:

a^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime};\left\{p\right\}_{n})\xrightarrow{u\to 0}\sum_{\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}}\sum_{ij,u}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)a^{0}_{n-1}(\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime};.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.),

(191)

which is capturing not only the soft behaviour of the considered colour correlation in all possible soft limits, but also the correct collinear behaviour in any collinear
g\parallel g
and
g\parallel q
configuration. In particular, we notice that the described procedure also retains IC collinear limits for a hard initial-state gluon and a final-state quark. This is straightforwardly achieved by letting the initial-state gluon play the role of the unresolved one, and then by replacing the eikonal factor with suitable IC IF or II antenna functions, which do not contain any soft limit.

Finally the treatment
q^{\prime}\parallel{\bar{q}}^{\prime}
collinear limits is quite simple, since these limits are only present in colour-interferences where
q^{\prime}
and
{\bar{q}}^{\prime}
are colour connected in both ordering
c
and
c^{\prime}
. In this case, the factorization reads:

a^{0}_{n}(c,c^{\prime};.,q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime},.)\xrightarrow{u\to 0}\dfrac{1}{s_{q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}}P^{0}_{q{\bar{q}}\to g}(x)\,a^{0}_{n-1}(\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime};.,\widetilde{q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}},.),

(192)

where
\widetilde{q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}
represent a gluon, which, in the colour orderings
\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
of the reduced matrix element, occupies the same positions of the original
q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}
pair. As far as the other hard partons are concerned,
\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
coincide with
c
and
c^{\prime}
. The splitting function
P^{0}_{q{\bar{q}}\to g}/s_{q^{\prime}{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}
can then be replaced by an appropriate antenna function choosing a spectator parton for the collinear limit. If the spectator is chosen to be a gluon
g
,
G_{3}^{0}(g,q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime})
is used, otherwise, if it is a quark
q
,
E_{3}^{0}(q,q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime})
is considered. Any choice yields the same result in the collinear limit, given that the spectator parton in these antenna functions is not associated with any singular behaviour.

We notice that the extraction of the single-unresolved behaviour of colour-ordered interferences discussed here is completely analogous to the description of infrared limits done in the context of the dipole subtraction formalism
Catani:1996jh
. We focused here on specific colour orderings, or equivalently specific directions in colour space, rather than on the full matrix element, in order to derive the correct factorization behaviour of a more generic object, namely
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

With the described procedure, we can systematically write down the singular behaviour of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in single-unresolved limits and consequently assemble
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

Pole cancellation

The outcome of the first part of the current derivation,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, has explicit
\epsilon
-poles, which need to be removed without spoiling the infrared behaviour of the whole expression.

We consider the generic structure of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which is given by
Currie:2013dwa
;
Chen:2022ktf
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij,u}\sum_{a,b}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\Big[\Big.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\phantom{-}\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},a)-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,a)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,j)\right)\Big.\Big]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,a,.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{ju},.,b,.),

(193)

where we chose to use integrated colour-stripped one-loop dipoles, but we could have equivalently used integrated three-parton tree-level antenna functions
\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}
, since the mass factorization kernels cancel exactly in the three differences of integrated dipoles. The combination above has residual
\epsilon^{-1}
poles, whose origin can be related to soft gluons emitted at large angle in the double-real emission correction. As discussed in
Weinzierl:2008iv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
, such poles cancel against a suitable combination of integrated soft eikonal factors. One can verify that the poles structure given by the arrangement of integrated dipoles in (
5.3.3
) is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Big\{\Big.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},a)-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,a)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,j)\right)\Big.\Big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left\{-\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{(iu)(uj)}}{s_{ij}}\right)+\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{(iu)a}}{s_{ia}}\right)+\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{b(uj)}}{s_{bj}}\right)\right\}.

(194)

We note that there are no residual poles in the
N_{f}
colour factor
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
. The poles structure above is removed introducing the so-called
integrated large angle soft terms

Weinzierl:2008iv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
;
Glover:2010kwr
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2011jwo
;
Currie:2013vh
. We consider the integral of an eikonal factor over the unresolved antenna phase space:

\mathcal{S}(s_{ij},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{ij,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})=\dfrac{8\pi^{2}}{\overline{C}(\epsilon)}\int\text{d}\Phi_{X_{iuj}}S^{0}_{iuj}.

(195)

In the expression above,
i
and
j
represent any pair of hard partons, while partons
i^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
can be chosen arbitrarily since there is a priori no singular behaviour associated with them. They appear as reference momenta in a phase space mapping for soft gluon radiation between the hard radiators
i
and
j
, which can, but do not have to, be different from
i^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
. All specific arrangements of
i,j
and
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
are discussed in Section
5.4.3
below, when these structures are propagated at the double-real level. In particular, for process involving four or more partons at LO,
i^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}
can be chosen to be two final-state partons
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2011jwo
. This is the reason why in (
195
) we used the FF antenna phase space. Explicit expressions for (
195
) are given in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2011jwo
. The key point for the current discussion is that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}oles\Bigg\{\Bigg.

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{ij},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},1)\right)

(196)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)a},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)a,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{ia},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{ia,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{b(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{b(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{bj},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{bj,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)\Bigg.\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=-\dfrac{1}{\epsilon}\left\{-\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{(iu)(uj)}}{s_{ij}}\right)+\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{(iu)a}}{s_{ia}}\right)+\ln\left(\dfrac{s_{b(uj)}}{s_{bj}}\right)\right\},

namely the pole structure of the arrangement of integrated eikonal factors above precisely cancels against the one in (
5.3.3
). The combination:

\displaystyle\Bigg[\Bigg.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,j)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},a)-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(i,a)\right)-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{uj})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,j)\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]

\displaystyle-\Bigg[\Bigg.\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{ij},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},1)\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)a},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)a,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{ia},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{ia,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{b(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{b(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{bj},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{bj,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]

(197)

is indeed free from explicit infrared singularities, for any partonic configuration. Therefore, the expression for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in (
5.3.3
) can be made
\epsilon
-finite by suitably inserting integrated eikonal factors according to the following replacements:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},\widetilde{uj})+\mathcal{S}(s_{ij},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},1)-\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}),

(198)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},a)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{iu},a)+\mathcal{S}(s_{ia},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{ia,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)a},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)a,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}),

(199)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{uj})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{uj})+\mathcal{S}(s_{bj},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{bj,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{b(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{b(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}).

(200)

We notice that the considered arrangement of integrated eikonal factors vanishes in single unresolved limits, therefore no additional unresolved behaviour is introduced. The complete
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is then obtained performing the substitutions above in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. For bookkeeping purposes, we denote as
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\mathcal{S}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
the collection of integrated eikonal factors we have included in the subtraction term. It has the following structure:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\mathcal{S}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim-\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\sum_{ij,u}\sum_{a,b}X_{3}^{0}(i,u,j)\Big[\Big.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\phantom{-}\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{ij},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},1)-\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{ia},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{ia,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{(iu)a},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{(iu)a,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\left(\mathcal{S}(s_{bj},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{bj,i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})-\mathcal{S}(s_{b(uj)},s_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}},x_{b(uj),i^{\prime}j^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\Big.\Big]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,a,.,\widetilde{iu},.,\widetilde{uj},.,b,.),

(201)

The procedure described up to here is sufficient to ensure finiteness in the soft gluon as well as in the
g\parallel g
and
q\parallel{\bar{q}}
collinear limits. In fact, it turns out that in such limits, the contribution of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is even suppressed by several orders of magnitude with respect to other components of the real-virtual subtraction term, due to a basically complete cancellation of the divergent behaviour in the RHS of (
182
). However, the strategy above requires further extension to handle also
q\parallel g
collinear limits.
Indeed, in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, constructed according to (
182
), contributions proportional to
A
- and
D
-type unintegrated antenna functions do not actually have the structure given in (
5.3.3
). In particular, they do not come with the complete set of three pairs of integrated dipoles, but rather with one or two pairs only. This is explained by the interplay of two phenomena. First of all, we have seen in Section
3.3.2
that it is necessary to introduce quark-antiquark integrated dipoles by means of the colour operator
\overline{\mathbfcal{J}}^{(2)}
to remove unphysical limits present in
D
-type antenna functions. Hence, a propagation of
A
-type antenna functions occurs from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
to the real-virtual subtraction term, which are not matched by corresponding structures coming from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. Additionally, the colour-charge operators for a quark-gluon dipole
\bm{T}_{q}\cdot\bm{T}_{g}
and quark-antiquark dipole
\bm{T}_{q}\cdot\bm{T}_{\bar{q}}
contribute to different colour factors when evaluated on a given tree-level or one-loop amplitude. Specifically,
\bm{T}_{q}\cdot\bm{T}_{\bar{q}}
does not contribute at leading-colour. This yields incomplete sets of integrated dipoles in (
5.3.3
) for some choices of hard partons
i
,
j
,
a
and
b
. The incompleteness of (
5.3.3
) prevents the removal of the leftover
\epsilon
-poles by the inclusion of the combination of integrated eikonal factors described above. However, according to our understanding, (
5.3.3
) is the only fundamental
\epsilon
-finite structure we can construct for arbitrary partonic configurations, which correctly propagates at the double-real level. The solution to this issue consists of restoring the complete structure in (
5.3.3
) by introducing suitable combinations of integrated dipoles, to then proceed with the inclusion of integrated eikonal factors as before. We illustrate how this is done in the following.

We consider a process with a colour-connected quark-antiquark pair denoted with
(q,{\bar{q}})
. We focus first on contributions proportional to
D
-type antenna functions, generically indicated in the following by
D_{3}^{0}(q,u,g)
, with
g
a hard gluon serving as the second hard radiator for the antenna, and
u
, as usual an unresolved gluon. The incomplete structure they come with has the following form:

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}D_{3}^{0}(q,u,g)\Bigg[\Bigg.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},\widetilde{ug})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,g)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{ug})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,g)\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{ug},.,b,.),

(202)

where
b
indicates a generic hard spectator different from
{\bar{q}}
. One can see that a pair of integrated dipoles is missing. To fix this, we can add the following term:

-\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}D_{3}^{0}(q,u,g)\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},{\bar{q}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right)a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,{\bar{q}}.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{ug},.,b,.),

(203)

with
{\bar{q}}
acting as a spectator along with
q
in quark-antiquark integrated dipoles. The resulting structure then reads

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}D_{3}^{0}(q,u,g)\Bigg[\Bigg.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},\widetilde{ug})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,g)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{ug})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,g)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},{\bar{q}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right)\Bigg.\Bigg]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,{\bar{q}}.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{ug},.,b,.),

(204)

which is analogous to (
5.3.3
).

We address now the case of an generic unintegrated
A
-type antenna function
A_{3}^{0}(q,u,{\bar{q}})
. In
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
it comes with a single pair of integrated dipoles:

\displaystyle\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}A_{3}^{0}(q,u,{\bar{q}})\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right)a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}},.),

(205)

hence we need to add two pairs of integrated dipoles:

\displaystyle-\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}A_{3}^{0}(q,u,{\bar{q}})\Big[\Big.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},q)-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,a)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,{\bar{q}})\right)\Big.\Big]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,a,.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}},.,b,.),

(206)

with
a
and
b
two hard spectators. Differently from the
D
-type antenna case, here there is no natural choice for spectators
a
and
b
and they can in principle be represented by any hard parton in the process different from
q
and
{\bar{q}}
. However, inconsistent choices of
a
and
b
across multiple contributions may produce subtraction terms which perform in a suboptimal way in some unresolved limits, either at the real-virtual or double-real level after insertion of a second unresolved parton. For specific partonic configurations or colour factors it is possible to define guidelines for the choice of
a
and
b
. For example, at leading-colour the choice is dictated by the adjacency patterns in the colour ordering of the reduced matrix elements. In general, the numerical validation of the subtraction terms, discussed in Section
6.1
, typically indicates which choice of spectators is preferable.

We collect all the inserted dipoles in (
203
) and (
5.3.3
) in a contribution that we denote as
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{extra}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{extra}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sum_{q,{\bar{q}},g,u}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}D_{3}^{0}(q,u,g)\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},{\bar{q}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,{\bar{q}})\right)a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,{\bar{q}}.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{ug},.,b,.)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sum_{q,{\bar{q}},u,a,b}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}A_{3}^{0}(q,u,{\bar{q}})\Big[\Big.\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(\widetilde{qu},q)-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(q,a)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\hskip 65.44142pt-\left(\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}})-\mathcal{J}_{2}^{(1)}(b,{\bar{q}})\right)\Big.\Big]a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,a,.,\widetilde{qu},.,\widetilde{u{\bar{q}}},.,b,.).

(207)

Adding
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{extra}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and removing of the residual poles with
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\mathcal{S}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
yields a finite and complete result. However,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{extra}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
inevitably introduces additional singularities in
g\parallel q
collinear limits, deviating from the original design of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. One finds that the divergent behaviour ends up being over-subtracted exactly twice, and simply adding an overall factor
1/2
to the final expression (after the removal of the
\epsilon
-poles) compensates for the additional terms. As we explained before, other single-unresolved limits are not affected by this, since there
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is vanishing by construction. The characteristic factor
1/2
derived here can be also explicitly found for example in
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2011jwo
;
Currie:2013dwa
. There, its presence was inferred at leading-colour proceeding with the traditional logic: from the double-real correction to the real-virtual one. The recovery of the same structures in the general colourful approach is a solid cross-check.

To summarize, the final expression for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{prel.}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\mathcal{S}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c,\text{extra}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(208)

with the different components given in equations (
182
), (
5.3.3
) and (
5.3.3
) respectively. We conclude the treatment of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
in the following section, illustrating which part of the structures discussed above is actually derived from the double-virtual subtraction terms via the insertion of an unresolved partons and which terms, on the contrary, constitute a new addition at the real-virtual level, which will be inherited by the double-real subtraction term.

Extraction of
d
\sigma^{T,c_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

The
\sigma^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
derived above contains contributions which can be related to structures in the double-virtual subtraction terms that underwent the insertion of an unresolved parton. These contributions are denoted as
\sigma^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}
and
\sigma^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

Currie:2013vh
;
Chen:2022ktf
.

The first block
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
comes from part of the leftover
+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]
that we did not use in Section
5.3.2
during the construction of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. In particular, it comes from the insertion of an unresolved parton in the colour-connected and almost colour-connected components of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
defined in equations (
150
) and (
151
):

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,a.c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right].

(209)

The remaining colour-unconnected component
+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,u.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]
produces structures in the double-real subtraction term, as we will explain in Section
5.4.4
.

To generate
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, we directly use the residual term from
177
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]\sim+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(210)

where the proportionality factor depends on the
m_{X}
required in
177
for the respective term.
We observe that, to ensure that the insertion is properly performed in both integrated antenna functions, the same symmetrization procedure employed for
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
can be used here as well.

We can now define the following term:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(211)

which collects all contributions in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
that are not derived from the unintegration of structures in the double-virtual subtraction term and, hence, are introduced at the real-virtual level for the first time. As we see in Section
5.4.3
, the insertion of an unresolved parton within
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
produces contributions in the double-real subtraction term.

5.3.4
Summary and observations

For ease of reference, we collect here the main results of this section. The real-virtual subtraction term has been decomposed according to:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

(212)

The first term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
has an NLO-like structure and is presented in section
5.3.1
. The other terms are given by

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(213)

with

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-2\,\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(214)

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(215)

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{3}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\beta_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(216)

and

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}},

(217)

with

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]+\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,a.c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(218)

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right]-\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},M_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right].

(219)

All the contributions to
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
are discussed in detail respectively in sections
5.3.2
and
5.3.3
.

To illustrate the origin of the different contributions,
we also recall the full decomposition of the double virtual subtraction terms, described in detail in section
5.2
:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,a_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

(220)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,a.c.c.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,c.u.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{3}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\beta_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

One can notice that almost the entire double-virtual subtraction term has been converted into its unintegrated counterpart and has been used at the real-virtual level, with the only exceptions being
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,b,c.u.}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which are directly converted to the double-real subtraction term. Moreover, new components had to be added at the real-virtual level and require a corresponding counterpart in the double-real subtraction term. These components are
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
,
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,c_{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
.

5.4
NNLO double-real subtraction term

The last ingredient for an NNLO calculation is the double-real subtraction term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, which removes the divergent behaviour of the double-real matrix element in single and double unresolved limits. In the colourful antenna subtraction approach, the generation of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is the last step of the procedure. It is a feature of the colourful formalism that
once the double-virtual and the real-virtual subtraction terms are available, it is quite straightforward to complete the subtraction procedure with the missing blocks needed to cancel the unmatched contributions in those two layers.

The double-real subtraction term is constructed by inserting a second unresolved parton in contributions coming from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
and two unresolved partons in terms coming from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. As we discuss below, the only genuinely new procedure at this level is represented by the simultaneous insertion of two colour-connected unresolved partons within the integrated four-parton antennae
\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}
.

We recall the usual decomposition of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}

Currie:2013vh
:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,d}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

(221)

The first term
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
removes single unresolved limits and it is analogous to an NLO real subtraction term for an underlying
(n+1)
-particle final state LO process. The remaining terms respectively reproduce the divergent behaviour of the double-real correction in colour-connected, almost colour-connected and colour-unconnected configurations
Glover:2010kwr
;
Currie:2013vh
.
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,c}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
also contains the large angle soft terms
Weinzierl:2008iv
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2007foh
. In the following, we describe how to systematically generate each contribution.

5.4.1
d
\sigma^{S,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

This contribution to the subtraction term is directly generated from
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, since it can be seen as its corresponding real NLO subtraction term:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\int_{1}\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}-\text{d}\hat{\sigma}^{MF,1,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}},

(222)

which reflects equation (
6
) and therefore, following what is done in Section
4.3
for the NLO real subtraction term, we can simply write:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,a}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right].

(223)

5.4.2
d
\sigma^{S,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}

In the colour-connected configuration, the two unresolved partons are emitted between the same pair of hard radiators. According to
Currie:2013vh
, we further decompose
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
into two contributions:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}+\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}.

(224)

The
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
terms contains four-parton antenna functions
X_{4}^{0}
, while the
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
term contains convolutions of two three-parton antenna functions
X_{3}^{0}\otimes X_{3}^{0}
, which are needed to remove the singular behaviour of
X_{4}^{0}
antenna functions in single-unresolved limits.

The generation of
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
is straightforward, since its integrated counterpart is exactly
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, so:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(225)

where the momenta relabelling due to the insertion of an unresolved parton must also occur within the unintegrated antenna functions which appear in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{T,b_{2},J_{X}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
. The general structure of the resulting term is the following:

\displaystyle\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{2}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\sim\sum_{ij,u_{1},u_{2}}\sum_{c,c^{\prime}}\Bigg[\Bigg.X_{3}^{0}(i,u_{2},j)X_{3}^{0}(\widetilde{iu_{2}},u_{1},\widetilde{ju_{2}})a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,\widetilde{(iu_{2})u_{1}},.,\widetilde{(u_{2}j)u_{1}},.)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+X_{3}^{0}(i,u_{2},u_{1})X_{3}^{0}(\widetilde{iu_{2}},\widetilde{u_{1}u_{2}},j)a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,\widetilde{(iu_{2})(u_{1}u_{2})},.,\widetilde{j(u_{1}u_{2})},.)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+X_{3}^{0}(u_{1},u_{2},j)X_{3}^{0}(i,\widetilde{u_{1}u_{2}},\widetilde{ju_{2}})a^{0}_{n+2}(c,c^{\prime};.,\widetilde{i(u_{1}u_{2})},.,\widetilde{(u_{2}j)(u_{1}u_{2})},.)\Bigg.\Bigg],

(226)

where
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
represent the two unresolved partons.

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
comes from the insertion of two unresolved partons in
\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}
, namely from the transition of integrated tree-level four-parton antenna functions to the unintegrated level. In Figure
2
, such insertion was depicted with two connected descendant red arrows to emphasize the fact that it cannot be performed relying on the
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
operator defined in Section
4
. Nevertheless, we can still identify a one-to-one correspondence between four-parton integrated and unintegrated antenna functions:

\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}(s_{ij})a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime},\{.,i,.,j,.\})\leftrightarrow X_{4}^{0}(i,u_{1},u_{2},j)a_{n+2}^{0}(c,c^{\prime},\{.,\widetilde{iu_{1}u_{2}},.,\widetilde{u_{1}u_{2}j},.\}).

(227)

The momentum mapping relating the two different phase-space multiplicities is the one appropriate at NNLO
Kosower:2002su
;
Gehrmann-DeRidder:2005btv
;
Currie:2013vh
. The simultaneous insertion of two unresolved partons cannot be achieved by the single-insertion operator
\mathcal{I}ns\left[\cdot\right]
. We need to define a new procedure, which is however analogous to the one described in Section
4
. The insertion of two unresolved colour-connected partons occurs as follows:

1)

remove the mass factorization kernels if present;

2)

replace each four-parton integrated antenna function
\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}(s_{ij})
with its unintegrated counterpart
X_{4}^{0}(i,u_{1},u_{2},j)
(see below);

3)

suitably replace the momenta in the colour interferences, according to the accompanying integrated antenna, following (
227
);

4)

apply the same momenta relabelling to the jet function;

5)

promote the phase-space measure to the appropriate one for
(n+2)
final-state momenta;

6)

replace the overall factor with appropriate one for the real correction:

\mathcal{N}^{VV}_{NNLO}\to\mathcal{N}^{RR}_{NNLO}=s_{RR}\left(4\pi\alpha_{s}\right)^{2}\mathcal{N}_{LO},

(228)

where
s_{RR}
compensates the potentially different final state symmetry factors in the presence two real emissions;

As for the single-insertion case, we need to clarify how exactly point
2
is performed. At NNLO we can identify the following possibilities for the insertion of two unresolved partons:

•

insertion of two unresolved gluons;

•

insertion of an unresolved quark-antiquark pair, which becomes soft or collinear to hard partons;

•

insertion of two collinear quark-antiquark pairs;

•

insertion of unresolved partons in IC limits.

We comment in the following about the different types of insertions. The appropriate replacement rules to convert integrated antenna functions to their unintegrated counterparts are presented in Appendix
A.2
.

Insertion of two unresolved gluons

The insertion of two unresolved gluons
u_{g_{1}}
and
u_{g_{2}}
was presented in
Chen:2022ktf
. It is the natural extension of the insertion of a single unresolved gluon. If the underlying LO process has
(n+2)
external partons, the additional gluonic emissions are labelled with indices
u_{g_{1}}=n+3
and
u_{g_{2}}=n+4
. The expressions obtained performing steps 1 to 4 above provides the appropriate structure to remove the double-unresolved limits of partons
u_{1}=n+3
and
u_{2}=n+4
, which we denote with:

f(p_{1},\dots,p_{n+2};p_{n+3},p_{n+4}),

(229)

with the last two entries representing the two momenta which can become unresolved. The ordering of the hard momenta
p_{1},\dots,p_{n+2}
is irrelevant, since a sum over all possible colour orderings is considered. A suitable sum of structures like the one in (
229
) is required to cover all possible two-gluon unresolved configurations of the double-real matrix elements:

\sum_{g_{1},g_{2}\,\in\,\text{f.s. gluons}}f(\dots;p_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}}),

(230)

where the sum runs over the momenta of ordered pairs of final-state gluons and the dots indicate all the other external momenta. The final result reproduces the full singular behaviour of the double-real matrix element when any pair of colour-connected gluons becomes unresolved. The replacement rules for the insertion of two unresolved gluons are given in Appendix
A.2
in Table
28
.

Insertion of an unresolved quark-antiquark pair

This type of insertion differs from the corresponding one at NLO because in the infrared limits it addresses, the extra quark-antiquark pair
(u_{q},u_{{\bar{q}}})
can become soft or collinear to another hard parton.

On the practical side, the insertion at NNLO is actually simpler to perform than the one at NLO. Indeed, at NLO, the splitting of a gluon into a quark-antiquark pair required a proper shift of all the partonic indices and a dedicated procedure to cover all possible splittings. At NNLO, given that this is a double-unresolved limit, we can proceed as for the insertion of two unresolved gluons and simply append the indices of the newly added quark and antiquark to the original set of momenta, namely
u_{q}=n+3
and
u_{\bar{q}}=n+4
for a process with
(n+2)
partons at LO. Moreover, no additional sum over partonic permutations is needed. The associated replacement rules are given in Appendix
A.2
in Table
29
.

Insertion of two unresolved quark-antiquark pairs

This insertion targets the situation in which two hard gluons individually split into two collinear quark-antiquark pairs
(u_{q},u_{\bar{q}})
and
(u_{q^{\prime}},u_{{\bar{q}}^{\prime}})
. Since the two pairs belong to distinct fermionic lines, their flavours can be considered different, with the same-flavour case not adding any contribution to this specific unresolved limit.

The only IP antenna function related to such configuration is the gluon-gluon final-final antenna function
H_{4}^{0}(q,{\bar{q}},q^{\prime},{\bar{q}}^{\prime})
, which only contains the double-collinear limit
q\parallel{\bar{q}}
,
q^{\prime}\parallel{\bar{q}}^{\prime}
. The transition from the integrated to the unintegrated antenna function is given in Appendix
A.2
in Table
30
. Symmetrization within each pair and over the exchange of the two pairs is considered. As it happens for the quark-antiquark insertion at NLO, besides adding two extra partons, two gluons which are present in the original set of partons need to be converted into quarks. We consider an
(n+2)
-parton process with
n_{g}\geq 2
final-state gluons labelled as
i,\dots,i+n_{g}-1
. After the insertions, the final-state gluons can be relabelled as
i,\dots,i+n_{g}-3
, while the added quarks are labelled, for example, as:

u_{q}=i+n_{g}-2,\quad\quad u_{\bar{q}}=i+n_{g}-1,\quad\quad u_{q^{\prime}}=n+3,\quad\quad u_{{\bar{q}}^{\prime}}=n+4.

(231)

The insertion is done by letting all possible pairs of external gluons split into the quark-antiquark pairs, so no additional sums are required to reproduce the full singular behaviour of the matrix element.

Insertion of two unresolved partons in IC limits

At NNLO, significantly more IC configurations are present with respect to the NLO case. The possibilities parallel the two-loop mass factorization kernels listed in Section
2.1.2
. The replacement rules to convert IC four-parton antenna functions to their unintegrated counterparts are given in Appendix
A.2
in Table
31
for quark-antiquark antenna functions, in Table
32
for quark-gluon antenna functions and in Table
31
for gluon-gluon antenna functions.

We introduce a new operator denoted with
\mathcal{I}ns_{2}\left[\cdot\right]
for the simultaneous double insertion of two unresolved partons, applying the procedure outlined above addressing each insertion possibility. The application of
\mathcal{I}ns_{2}\left[\cdot\right]
is summarized in Figure
4
.

Removal of splitting kernels

Insertion of two unresolved partons:
\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}\to X_{4}^{0}

Relabelling/mapping of momenta

Fix overall factors

Figure 4
:
Application of the
\mathcal{I}ns_{2}\left[\cdot\right]
operator for the simultaneous insertion of two colour-connected unresolved partons.

With the new operator, we can finally write:

\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{S,b_{1}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}=-\mathcal{I}ns_{2}\left[\text{d}{\hat{\sigma}^{U,c,\mathcal{X}_{4}^{0}}_{ab,\mathrm{NNLO}}}\right],

(232)

which has the following structure:
